a giant circle frames NIKO’s timber guesthouse and tiny curved sauna in japan

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

NIKO design studio's 'marumi house' in japan features a giant circular facade opening, exposed cedar logs, and a tiny sauna.

NIKO Design Studio has completed Umino Marumi, a two-story wooden guesthouse on a hillside overlooking the Pacific coast of Chiba, Japan. With its enormous circular facade opening and exposed timber structure, the house makes an unusual addition to the surrounding residential neighborhood. In the garden, a much smaller companion building takes shape as a sauna hut, its curved roof designed to accommodate the trees growing around it.

Completed in 2025, Marumi was conceived as a second home where the owners could gather with family and friends, while also creating a welcoming presence within the local community. The architects approached this ambition through an engawa, the traditional Japanese veranda, transforming it into a series of staggered outdoor platforms that climb through the house and connect to the upper floor.

The Marumi House’s most recognizable feature is the enormous circular opening cut into its dark facade, revealing a network of timber columns and raised walkways beneath the pitched roof. Around one-third of the covered building is dedicated to the semi-outdoor engawa, where visitors can sit, gather, or move between levels without entering the enclosed rooms.

With this layout, the architects at NIKO Design Studio give the house an unusually open relationship with its surroundings. From outside, the veranda offers glimpses of people moving through the timber structure, while its various levels accommodate both intimate gatherings and larger groups. The circular opening brings these activities into view, turning the facade into an inhabited space that faces the neighborhood.

NIKO Design Studio worked with substantial cedar logs measuring approximately 400 millimeters in diameter, assembling the structural frame using traditional kanuki joinery. Horizontal timber members pass through openings in the round columns, creating a durable structure that retains the natural geometry of the wood. The holes were cut using chainsaws, following an approach suggested by the carpenters and inspired by forestry techniques.

The same hands-on approach extends into the landscape. To address the sloping site, a stone retaining wall was assembled during a two-day workshop using nozumi, a traditional stone-stacking technique. The rough stonework sits alongside the large timber structure, which brings a sense of craftsmanship to a building designed for communal use.

Beyond the main house, the architects faced a different challenge when designing a freestanding sauna for the garden. The owners initially wanted a cabin for three or four people, but commercially available models appeared surprisingly bulky when placed within a model of the site. Looking more closely at the surrounding landscape, the team noticed a neighboring palm tree extending over the property boundary. Its continued growth became the starting point for the sauna’s unusual curved roof.

Working with carpenter Murakami of Murakami Carpentry Workshop, the architects sourced naturally bent timber from trees that had grown along a hillside. These curved members formed the roof structure, allowing its profile to rise and dip around the surrounding vegetation. When the timber arrived thicker and longer than anticipated, the team decided to retain the excess length, leaving the beams projecting beyond the roofline. The unexpected detail gives the small building much of its character.

Despite its expressive roof, the sauna occupies remarkably little ground. Its foundation measures just two by two meters, with a total floor area of five square meters including the stepped portion. Inside, timber-lined surfaces and a tall ceiling make the small space feel surprisingly generous. Although originally intended for three or four bathers, the completed sauna can accommodate five or six people.

The hut combines electric and wood-fired heating, while a skylight brings daylight into the compact interior. It connects with the surrounding terrace and the house’s bathroom, which can be a cold bath during sauna sessions. Marumi and its miniature companion together explore two scales of wooden architecture, with one opening itself toward the neighborhood and the other adjusting its form to the trees already growing in the garden.

Michèle Mouton Driving A Quattro S1 Up Pikes Peak Is The Coolest Way To Promote Audi's New Q7

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The first thing that comes to mind when you mention Quattro is probably Audi's 1980 Group B rally car. It makes perfect sense that the German manufacturer would lean on that legacy when promoting its new Q7 and SQ7 SUVs that have the trademarked all-wheel-drive system, but the company did something much cooler for its new ad campaign: Audi reunited Michèle Mouton with a Quattro S1 at the iconic Pikes Peak hill climb. The 1982 World Rally Championship runner-up still looked at home behind the wheel of the 493-horsepower rally monster.

Audi's new ad campaign titled "Performance Runs in the Family" features a pair of videos: a typical 30-second TV spot, and a 4-minute short with Mouton driving a Quattro S1 up Pikes Peak. During the visually stunning drive up the Colorado mountain, she reminisced about the animosity she faced during the 1985 hill climb, her winning run, and how the ascent has changed in the 41 years since.

Mouton was the first woman and the first non-American to win the Pikes Peak International Hill Climb. The Frenchwoman set a record time of 11:25.390 up the treacherous 12.4-mile ascent, besting the previous record set by Al Unser Jr. in 1983 by 13 seconds. Mouton is still effortlessly cool, still delivering pointed one-liners as naturally as the rest of us breathe. During the revival run, she noted, "When I was at this spot in the race, I had no time for feelings."

The 30-second TV spot mixes archival footage of Mouton during the Group B era with footage of a 2027 Audi SQ7 driving up Pikes Peak. To emphasize the campaign's legacy theme, the ad features Mouton alongside her daughter, Jessica. The third-generation Q7 isn't as nimble on the twisting mountain roads as a Group B rally car, but that isn't the point. The SUV's new styling looks just as aggressive and far more comfortable for a family. Vito Paladino, Audi of America's President, stated in a release:

"Audi's story of quattro didn't end with rally racing. That connection between performance and everyday capability is what makes quattro such a defining part of our brand. The all-new Audi Q7 family carries that legacy forward, bringing together Audi's performance heritage with the innovation and engineering that define the next generation of our vehicles."

The 1985 Pikes Peak International Hill Climb was Mouton's last event with Audi before she departed for her farewell season with Peugeot. The Frenchwoman and her accomplishments will forever be tied to Audi. Mouton's four WRC victories with the Audi Quattro are a legacy every four-wheel-drive model produced by the German automaker inherits. It's not merely laurels to rest on, but a level of performance that Audi has to honor.

Why Blake Griffin Couldn’t Leave Basketball Alone

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Twelve years ago, I was sitting in Blake Griffin’s house, listening to him tell me that under no circumstances would he stay around basketball after he retired. “Well, look who’s a liar,” he says now.

Griffin is a former NBA All-Star who played for 14 years, many of them for the Clippers, before announcing his retirement in 2024. He now has his own show, The Stepback with Blake Griffin, and a weekly gig with NBA on Prime, as part of their studio show. “I always thought that there was a little bit too much negativity when it came to scrutinizing players,” Griffin says. “This is a job that 400-something guys have. It’s extremely hard. I don’t need to go on TV and I don’t need to tell everybody that the guy that went three for 17 sucks at basketball. He doesn’t.”

On The Stepback, Griffin puts on a suit, sits behind a desk, and addresses a big, sports-related topic each week: the media, taxpayer-financed stadiums, the rocketing costs of youth sports, and so on. Like him, the show is unexpectedly funny, frequently insightful, and a good hang. In this conversation, he and I discuss the Clippers then and now, retiring at 34, and what it was like to have some rude journalist visit his house, all those years ago.

The following conversation is lightly edited and condensed from the latest episode of One More Question with Zach Baron. Watch below or on YouTube. Listen on Spotify or Apple Podcasts.

Blake Griffin: It’s funny, being 37 now, looking back at that time, and being like: “Man, I didn’t know what I was doing.” I can’t believe people were entrusting me with having a house and a car and doing all these adult things. I wasn’t an adult yet.

Well, in fairness to me, I’d only been living there for four years—no, I think it was fairly new at the time. I moved into that house at the end of 2013 and we spoke the next year. So over time you collect more stuff, figure out your taste.

He walked me through the entire party for—it felt like an hour, I don’t know, I had lost track of time. When you’re holding a 70-something-year-old man’s hand who’s not related to you, being introduced to party guests—and I think I was 19 at the time, I’d just gotten drafted—time sort of slows down. So I don’t know how long it was. Could have been half a day. But yeah, from the first time I met him, it was a very different experience.

Everybody, when I first moved there, was like, “Where are you from?” I’m like, “From Oklahoma.” And they’re like, “Oh, so—horses?” I’m like, “Well, yeah, but also cars.” It’s a civilized place. Culture shock in just that—I didn’t live in Hollywood, but you’re just in LA and that’s the epicenter of so much. It was a lot, but also you’re so focused when you’re 19. Apartment, practice facility, airport—those are the three things that I did. So it kind of gave me time to grow up. And then when we met, I was a much more seasoned man at 24, 25. It’s crazy looking back, because you’re like, “Man, I can’t believe I moved there at 19.” I tell people all the time, I grew up in LA.

I didn’t really dwell on that question. I don’t know. It was cool. I really enjoyed our conversation.

No, it’s fine. I was going there on my own. It’s fine. I’m happy to give one. I did. I enjoyed our conversation. I was actually thinking about it on the way here. Those are the best conversations—when you can just sit and talk and there’s not just these six questions that you’ve got to hit. You just let the conversation go where it goes. That’s what the conversation felt like to me.

Well, look who’s a liar. You know what’s crazy is, I never envisioned myself doing the NBA on Prime Show. And even until I retired and had conversations about going to join TNT or ESPN, whatever it was, and I still didn’t want to. And it really was Amazon getting a part of the TV rights and them really wanting to do a show differently than what was already on air. And that intrigued me because I thought there was this space in the NBA particularly where you can just analyze games, you can talk about basketball. And I always thought that there was a little bit too much negativity when it came to scrutinizing players. This is a job that 400 something guys have. It’s extremely hard. I don’t need to go on TV and I don’t need to tell everybody that the guy that went three for 17 sucks at basketball. He doesn’t. He had an off night. Instead, maybe I can walk you through some of the things he maybe could have done differently or some of the things he did well and it just wasn’t his night.

It’s kind of how I talk just in real life. I don’t want to harp on somebody who’s going through a shooting slump. Trust me, no one in the world wants to shoot better than the guy who’s in a shooting slump. It happened to me. Trust me. So I don’t need to come on TV and talk about it. So it’s kind of the same way I talk to people just in real life about certain games or what happened with this, what happened with that? It doesn’t always have to be negative.

I still do. I think that the day that I am bitter, or the day that I watch the game and I only see it from a negative standpoint or I’m like, “Back in our day, we didn’t do this or these guys are getting paid too much money”—I think that’s probably the day that I don’t need to do this anymore, because if I’m not going to adapt to where the game is today, I think that’s where you end up being jaded and you end up being biased in your observation or your analysis. And that’s what we’re hired to do, give our analysis. But it’s also like: Give your analysis on basketball today. It doesn’t matter what happened back then. And so my biggest thing too is every year I try to spend time with coaches, GMs, whoever it is, to keep understanding where the game is going, because it does change. Basketball evolves and you have to evolve your mindset around it.

We’re actually boys now. We went and did this golf thing together. I can’t lie. I was always just like, “What? Why? Give me a break, man.” I never was like, “Gosh, Charles is awesome.” Then you spend time with him and you see who he is and I appreciate it. I wasn’t the only person and at times it’s also not unwarranted. There’s games I played bad, I could have done better and this and that. And if that’s his style and that’s how he does it, that’s totally fine. It’s not that you can’t be critical. I just think I wanted to do [it] a little different.

There are definitely guys like that. My advice, if you’re in that spot, or something I wish somebody had told me when I was in this transition of really trying to develop my game, but also I was known for being aggressive, athletic, attack the rim, is you can continue to develop this skill and you continue to work on your game, but don’t go away from the things that got you to this place in the first place. So I would say to guys, use your athleticism, get to the rim, put pressure on the rim and then let that be your play making because now people have to rotate over, they have to help. Now you can open up different parts of your game. So a lot of times players hear all this like, “He can’t do this, he can’t do that.” And sometimes you kind of block out what you do well and you’re just so focused on what people are saying you can’t do.

Yeah. I mean, it’s almost impossible not to. I wouldn’t read tweets. I don’t read mentions during the season especially, but I wouldn’t put a lot of thought into comments, but it’s hard not to... When you’re just watching sports and you love watching sports and there’s morning shows on and it’s all around you, you’re going to hear stuff. And then there’s also the side of you that you know what you need to get better at as well.

I watched the game a little bit differently. I think as a player, I was watching so much of player tendencies, like I’m going to be guarding this guy in a couple days, or whatever it is. You’re also watching plays, you’re seeing teams run different sets. And now I’m interested a little bit more in the chess match that’s happening because trying to analyze, you’re looking at, okay, “What are the matchups? What would I have maybe done differently in that matchup? What could they do differently? What worked well? What didn’t work well?” And then you’re watching both sides of the ball, obviously. So it’s a lot of the same obviously basketball viewing, but I think when you’re trying to watch from an analyst perspective, you have to be a little bit more all-encompassing. When I’m watching as a player at night, if we don’t have a game, I’m not really tuning into, “Oh, since so-and-so got a tech, they’ve been on a 7-0 run,” but that’s something you clock now because it’s part of telling the story of the game.

I think it was my time. I think when I got to the point where I still enjoyed the game of basketball, but I was enjoying less and less everything else that came with it. My last several years I was in Detroit, I was in Brooklyn, I was in Boston, and my kids are in school in Los Angeles. So being away from them was tough. Even getting to see them once a month is still a little bit brutal. Or you’re on the road, you come back here, you get a couple days. So that wore on me. I also had an unfortunate amount of injuries, and I felt like I was having to put just as much in just to practice and then recover from that practice and then get ready for the game the next day, and it became this whole thing. I felt like I wanted to walk away from basketball on my own terms. I had a chance to go play another year and took some time to think about it, and I just felt like it was my time.

I don’t think there was a specific moment. I did remember when training camp came back around that next season and I was still trying to decide and I realized I didn’t feel like I was missing anything. I was like, “Oh, all right. Not as scary as I thought it would be.” Then as the season went on, I just didn’t see myself going back. I remember I was playing golf with JJ Redick once before I retired. This was before he took the job with the Lakers, and he said something to me that just stuck with me. He was like, “I’m telling you, you won’t be truly happy until you retire.” It kind of shocked me. Because he and I played together for five years, I think, and we were so similar in the way we prepared and the way we worked and how much we cared about the game that it just is this all-encompassing, obsessive thing. And it’s hard to really appreciate the little things, and it’s hard to appreciate making your own schedule. The school drop-offs, first day of school, doing all these different things. That’s sort of the way I took it, and the way I think that he meant it—is it’s such a grind, mental grind, physical grind, of putting literally everything you have into this thing. And then when you’re done, you just find this new sense of peace.

It’s amazing. I mean, I hope that they understood why I was having to be gone so much, but just being present is such a huge, huge gift. I mean, literally yesterday, my son had a football game that was two hours outside of LA. I drove two hours to watch an hour and a half football game, and then two hours back, and I was so happy. Now their games are what brings me sports joy.

It’s definitely scary because it’s also the one thing that you’ve done your whole life. And you also have to think—I dropped out of college. A lot of guys don’t even go, or guys are maybe there one year, and you don’t get the college experience of the workload and classes and graduating, internships, your first foray into the working world and you’re learning all these lessons and figuring out who you’re going to be, who you want to be, or how to operate in that world, and you miss out on that, and then you retire. I think a lot of guys have that What now? thing. And for me personally, doing nothing was never an option. I would be bored out of my mind. So that’s one thing I always preach to younger guys is find something you think you may want to do before you’re done.

Even in the offseason, you have to work out. So it’s still all based around this one thing. So even your free time is not quite necessarily like, “Okay, all right, go do whatever you want.” And then the season is so structured and you don’t really think about it. Every move is planned. You have your schedule in advance so you know it. It’s not like a surprise, but there’s no free time. I remember when I got traded to the Pistons, somebody asked me: “So, do you just commute? Are you still living in LA?” And I’m like, “No, no. We play every other day. There’s no commute time.”

You definitely have those moments. I kind of made this thing where whenever you get injured—unfortunately I had several times to perfect this—but I would give myself 24 hours to be upset, mad, angry, whatever, feel sorry for yourself. And then after that, you just have to put everything you have into coming back. But there was a specific one. I tore a ligament on the bottom of my foot in the playoffs in 2017. And I remember that season, I had a chef to make sure all my meals were exactly what I needed. I took my pregame naps in a hyperbaric chamber. I would nap in a hyperbaric chamber on off days, ice baths, hot tub, hot, cold, sauna. I was doing everything possible. And I remember the doctor coming in telling me that I tore my tendon, I had to have surgery, and I just broke down. And that was really the first time I really had a break. The other times it’s a shitty feeling, but I broke down, emotionally broke down. I just remember thinking, “Why me?” And I don’t know. I think I just allowed myself to have that moment, and then you have to just flip the switch and go into surgery and go into rehab and go into that whole process of coming back to the court with everything. Everything you would put into preparing for games, you have to put into that. You have to find a way. And for the most part, I feel like coming back from injuries, I did a pretty solid job. It was kind of like the last one. I felt like: I can’t get back to that certain level.

It’s a weird negotiation you’re having with your body like, “Come on, we’ve always done this. Let’s do it.” It actually is funny because you’re like, “Come on, him? He can’t stop you. Let’s go.” And your body’s just like, “Relax. I’ll tell you what we can do.” But I mean, you know it’s coming. Everybody—I mean, LeBron, I don’t even want to talk about, he’s a freak, guy’s a freak—but you know it’s coming and you just have to make that transition as graceful as possible. I feel like as I got older and when I got to that point, it’s like, Okay, you have more time to put more of yourself into your teammates, into making sure everybody else is good. And it sounds like you’re doing this selfless thing, but that’s part of how you contribute and come to terms with it. There’s different ways to be a team player.

I don’t know. It’s hard to answer that question. I think the thing that I am happy with is I know I worked as hard as I possibly could. To use a sports analogy, I left it all out there on the floor. When I was able to walk away, that’s part of the reason—because like I told you, I was doing everything possible. So when you feel like you laid it all out there and you gave it your true best shot, I think it was easier for me to walk away than to be at the end of my career and be like, “Yeah, but what if I had done this or what if I had done that?”

I mean, there’s definitely things where I’m just like, “I wish I had known this or I wish I had known that.” Earlier in my career, I remember Chris Paul always talking to me about screening angles and this and that. He’s talking about the strategy of how he wants to screen, or what I should be doing. It’s not that I was hearing that and being like, “No, no, I know how to do it.” It was like, you couldn’t quite grasp it until you get it. And as an older player, you start to understand these things better and they become second nature. So you’re not having to think through everything. And so now I’ll see an older clip or an older game and I’m like, “My screen should have been…” Stuff like that where it’s like, “Man, I wish I could go back and tell my younger self.” But then it’s also part of that thing where sometimes you have to experience and you have to understand it on your own. You can tell somebody how to do it, but until it clicks, it doesn’t work.

I miss games, especially the end of the fourth quarter of a close game when I’m watching. I’m like, “Oh man, that looks fun.” But then I’ll get a FaceTime from a buddy who’s still playing and he’s in three degree weather in Chicago on Tuesday in January and he’s going to shootaround and you’re like, “Oof, I don’t know if I miss that part.” So there’s the perspective. Like I said, the games were just the best. They’re the best. And then as I got older, some of the things that came along with it, I think that’s what I wasn’t really prepared for. When I wasn’t excited to do that for a full season, I think that’s when I knew it was time to step away.

I had the idea for this show a long time ago. I was watching John Oliver in 2016, and John Oliver’s the way I make sense of real world issues, politics, whatever’s going on. And it also keeps me entertained and locked in with the comedy aspect. And also always watching these reactionary debate shows, morning TV and basketball or sports in general, and realizing that there’s sort of a void in sports entertainment where it feels like you’re learning about some of these bigger issues that affect fans, sports fans, athletes, and even honestly not fans. I think one of my favorite things was talking to somebody this week who was not really a sports fan. They were like, “Hey, that episode is really cool. I didn’t even think about any of that.” We have a research room, we have a writer’s room, and we’re making sure that I’m not just spewing my personal thoughts or feelings about certain topics, but doing the research and saying, “Hey, I played sports and some of this stuff is still confusing to me, so let me do the research. I will present it to you and you can form your own opinion.” I thought there was a void for that.

I mean, there’s tons of podcasts. There’s so many athlete podcasts and they’re great. I am a listener of some, but like I said, I wanted this to be scripted in the way that you can trust that this is researched information and not just me being like, “Today, on The Blake Griffin Podcast, I’m going to talk about sports journalism. You know what? I got a bone to pick with Stephen A. Smith,” and just going at it from just my perspective.

I mean, one of the best parts about making this show is I felt like I got to utilize a research team to learn about all these topics because the idea of the show isn’t like, “Hey, I played sports. I know what I’m talking about.” It’s, “I played sports, I still don’t really know some of these things.” So being able to try to make sense of that from my perspective was the idea. But man, even just taking a step back to look at analytics and it’s like, “All right, when did this start?” And that’s sort of the entry point to that episode is like, “All right, this is how it started. This is what it’s become, and this is what I think is maybe the issue with it, and this is how we can move forward.” We tried to make every episode not just this like, “Hey, here’s an issue, this sucks.”

The “debate over everything” show was definitely something I was aware of. We were sitting in the training room one time and First Take was on, and it was like Stephen A. Smith and Skip screaming at each other, and the topic was our team, and guys are dying laughing because they’re both just dead wrong. It’s not like one guy you’re like, “Oh, he’s got a good point there.” You’re like, “What are they talking about?” And that was the other thing that sparked my idea for the show was there needs to be something where you can feel like you can rely on what’s being said.

I haven’t heard from him. Here’s my pushback on that. I’m hard on specific things that he said. I’m giving you a very real example, something that actually happened and then talking about it—that to me is a little bit different.

That’s a sports media story. So many guys have found out through Twitter what is going to happen to them in their career. I just feel like in this day and age where information is just about getting it out there first, we’ve sort of maybe lost a little bit of decency in how people are being affected by that. And yeah, you can say, These guys get to play sports and they get to make money doing the sport they love. But at the end of the day, I think you should always know first what’s happening in your life rather than everybody else.

Yeah. And I think that the insider can still be a thing. My argument in the sports journalism episode isn’t that like, “Hey, we need to get rid of this.” My argument is that if this is going to exist, we also need to make sure that we keep a space for real journalism.

I just wish that I had known and then there was just an upfront conversation like, “Hey, this is what we’re doing.” I remember after the trade, I remember my agent telling me, he was like, “I got calls from several GMs being like, ‘Why didn’t you tell us we would’ve…’” And it maybe was a situation closer to home. I truly enjoyed my time in Detroit. I really did. So it’s not the fact that I wish I could go back and go somewhere else or do something different. It’s just you just wish that there had been a conversation first.

Abraham Lincoln, obviously. Albert Einstein. Yeah, it was part of the free agency pitch.

I didn’t even process it when I first saw it. We’d had this free agency meeting with the front office and myself, my agent, my brother, and I come downstairs in Staples Center, Staples at the time, and all the staff is around and they all had these shirts on and I’m just saying hi. I mean, it’s people that I’ve known for nine years or eight years at the time. I was just saying hi and I kind of clocked the shirt, but I didn’t start looking at each person being like, “Wait, why did we do this?” I kid you not, said hello to everybody, got in the car, was on the 10 driving home, and the picture of the shirt was everywhere. So it was like 15, 20 minutes later, the shirt was already out there and people were dying. And then I was like, “Oh my gosh, that is nuts.” I didn’t even clock it in real time.

Yeah. And I’ve since talked to Lawrence Frank, and that was even something he expressed to me too, and zero hard feelings.

Yeah, [I] was definitely bummed about that too. I mean, knowing that was his last year and him going back to the Clippers—that’s a thing that a lot of guys do. It’s like, “I want to finish my career in a place that means a lot to me.” That was tough to see.

It’s been great. We have had a lot of conversations recently, and I think we all look back on that time of—Clippers in general, but that time in general also has a spot in our heart. We had a great time. Unfortunately, we didn’t win a championship. But I think we definitely have a sense of pride.

I definitely look back with a sense of pride. I think with the organization, when I got drafted to the Clippers, they were owned by Donald Sterling, who we touched on—historically bad franchise, it was a punchline. And over the course of eight years of being there, we got Chris Paul, we got JJ, we got all these guys, and we were making runs at not only just getting to the playoffs, but certain years I felt like we had a legitimate chance to win a championship. And obviously we ultimately came short, but I look back on it with a sense of pride more than anything, of trying to leave a place better than you found it. And they continued that success even after we left, and it was always a thing where you look back and you’re like, “It’s cool that we were sort of a team that ushered in that era of not being necessarily a punchline.”

I think I had answered a question that was asked of me and I wasn’t like, “It’s crazy they haven’t done…” It was just kind of like, “We haven’t gone back.” But it’s not a thing. I don’t take the stance of we deserve our time or our time to—it’s not really that. Like I said, if we had won a championship and there was never any recognition of that, sure. I mean, maybe you could be hurt about that, but yeah, that wasn’t a statement of like, “I can’t believe they haven’t had it.” That’s not how I feel at all.

No, I’ll carry the flag, man. I’ll carry the flag. I mean, I’ve said it time and again, Clippers fans here are an incredibly loyal bunch. I don’t think anybody can argue that. They’ve been through a lot of ups and downs, and I love it when I’m out in LA and a Clippers fan comes up to me like, “Hey man, I had Lob City tickets” or “I used to watch you play.” It always means something to me. It’s special. So I don’t shy away from being known as a Clippers player at all.

It’s obviously an unfortunate event. I feel bad for the guys that were in the trade coming to the Clippers. Or not necessarily bad, but if you’re a guy who’s 30, 31—Brandon Ingram, for example, he’s 29 and has been to several different places, a great player still, and now you’re coming to a place that doesn’t have draft picks for the next five years. And it’s not to say he can’t go anywhere else after his contract, but it feels like they were penalized and now those guys just kind of had no say and they’re just on that team and they’re like, “But I didn’t do anything.” And then also the fans, just having to sit through the next five years. And it’s not to say they can’t be competitive. I think they will be competitive. They have a great coach and they have solid pieces on their roster, but it’s hard to move forward as a franchise if you’ve been stripped of five picks. As far as: Did the punishment fit the crime? It’s not really even for me to say, to be honest.

Not really. I think teams will introduce a player’s agent to a company, but then the whole thing is—in an endorsement deal, who’s to say what you’re worth, in my opinion? If Aspiration wants to pay Kawhi, whatever it is, that’s on the company. But now if you’re not doing anything for it, huge red flag. I feel like if you had just planted one tree, just one tree—a bush, have a bonsai tree in your house, anything, I feel like that’s where you could just be like, “All right. I guess that’s what they wanted to pay.” So I think that sure, teams do introduce players to brands, but if you’re doing an endorsement deal and you are doing something for it, shooting a commercial, photo shoot, autograph signing, whatever it is, you can’t really argue.

No, I’ve never heard of a player in my time in the league that was just getting paid by this company to not do anything. Never have.

I prefer the basketball, but I think that’s part of it. I mean, there’s always a story. There’s always something going on. And especially now with players being their own brands and having such huge followings on social media, little tiny things can just become their own thing and it’ll grow on its own. I think it’s part of why basketball is so culturally relevant, but I prefer just the basketball part.

It’s funny because earlier you said, “You could have gone the podcast route and you did it the hard way.” And on top of that, I’m like, “Let’s do late night, that thing that’s definitely dying right now.” So, double down on the hardness of it. But I don’t think you’ve seen these shows go away because they’re just a dead format. I think that some of these shows, it’s budget and expense. Like this show, for example, the budget is not huge at all. I mean, I don’t even know what a regular late night budget is, but I would bet a 50th of that. So figuring out ways to do that format, do the late night format, include sports in today’s world. And thankfully YouTube allows people to do that. It’s one of the best things about it, is creators get to just create what they want. If you can figure out a way to take that format, the old school format and make it work in today’s world, today’s budget, I think that’s how you get things to work.

“Is it hard to date?” I’m married now. Not hard at all, actually. It’s been pretty chill.

Well, for one, for budget reasons, for other reasons, we had to block shoot these. So I shot 14 episodes and we had to keep things pretty evergreen and make sure that we weren’t dating ourselves. So eventually I’d love to get that shooting window from shoot to air much closer. I think season two will accomplish that, but eventually I’d love it to be more of a weekly style show where it’s still broader topics, well researched, scripted, but we’re able to have multiple episodes going at one time. And then if we need to activate one, we can and be able to have a much closer to Last Week Tonight, that format. So it’s truly up to date. I really enjoyed doing this. It was so much fun. As hard as it was and as much work as it was, I had a blast. It’s something that I truly, truly enjoyed. So as long as that joy’s there, I’d love to do it till the wheels fall off.

Why the Adobe MAX 2026 opening keynote has me feeling cautiously hopeful.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Adobe MAX is in full swing, and after this morning's keynote, I'm left feeling pleasantly surprised. I initially arrived with slight apprehension, expecting to leave fatigued and underwhelmed by the grand kickoff, especially regarding Adobe's ever-divisive AI developments. Instead, I must confess, I'm cautiously hopeful.

It's no secret Adobe is going full send on AI, after a full fleet of AI partnerships and Firefly taking centre stage, blossoming into a full-fledged creative studio. It could all start to feel a little existential, but beaming under the stage lights, future Adobe CEO Anil Chakravarthy assures me, "Adobe will meet you where you are”. And I start to believe him.

AI has persisted as a divisive topic among creatives, but Adobe has shown a refreshing self-awareness around the tension. Emerging on stage, vice president Deepa Subramaniam wastes no time addressing that "things feel uncertain” in the wake of AI.

Assurances of “reliability” and “safety” are thrown around, honing in on Adobe's legacy as a brand shaped and "built by creatives that care about the craft”. She assures the sceptics among the crowd (myself included) that for Adobe, "Creativity isn’t a side project; it’s a way of life,” and to my surprise, the typically eye-roll-worthy sentiment soothes me. At its core, it seems Adobe hasn't forgotten about the creatives that shaped its brand.

Deepa raises some valid points. In an increasingly content-driven world, more is asked of us. The humble graphic designer is now expected to be a web designer, motion designer, and social media maverick all in one – something that Adobe's “rich learning ecosystem” can solve, according to Deepa. Creatives are trapped in a battle with waning time and resources, with demand for content ever increasing. If handled correctly, time-saving AI could offer the respite we need, but it raises a moral dilemma. Where do we stop?

For more Adobe news, check out why Adobe's Google partnership hints at a radically different future for Photoshop or take a look at the new AI features coming to Photoshop.

‘It’s Like Facebook Again’: How AI IPOs Could Further Upend the Real Estate Market

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A robotic hand presents a house icon with a percentage symbol, symbolizing growth and investment in a modern office space, merging technology with real estate. Synapse

(TNS)– The deluge of wealth the artificial intelligence boom has brought into San Francisco has already pushed luxury home prices to record heights. Now, with Anthropic’s initial public offering poised to bring even more money into the city, could things get even worse?

Real estate professionals say yes—but that a lot of the growth is already baked in.

Let’s take stock of what’s already happened. San Francisco’s home prices have risen most sharply in neighborhoods that were already exclusive, such as Pacific Heights or the Marina. The median home price for luxury homes in the San Francisco metropolitan area—properties sold in San Francisco and San Mateo counties at or above the 95th percentile price—was nearly $7.5 million from July to September 2026, Redfin data shows. That was up a whopping 23% from the same period in 2025, and up more than 60% from 2019.

Now, some experts suggest that AI IPOs could cause demand to spill even further, raising prices for the few home types—or communities—in the Bay Area where middle-income families might still afford a property.

Already, some data indicates that even non-luxury homes in the city have seen strong appreciation over the past year.

“Typical” San Francisco area homes—those in the 35th-to-65th percentile sale price range — saw a record median sale price of $1.6 million in September 2026, an increase of 6.5% from 2025. While that’s a much lower jump compared to the San Francisco area’s luxury market, it still diverges sharply from that of the rest of the United States, where high mortgage rates and economic woes have caused prices to dip.

Redfin economist Daryl Fairweather believes the heat in San Francisco will spread to other parts of the region. In some ways, it already has; the city’s surging rents have caused many tenants to seek refuge in relatively more affordable cities like Oakland, pushing up prices there.

“This has been the story for at least … 15 years: San Francisco prices and rents going up and people being displaced,” Fairweather said.

Demand for fancy homes will likely keep surging

Some real estate agents say that the AI workers and investors fighting bidding wars in San Francisco often don’t want to give up on their dream house, even as prices rise. That’s part of the reason demand has flowed into other expensive cities in Marin County or the Peninsula, where there are more fancy homes for the buying. Phil Chen, a real estate broker at Christie’s International Real Estate Sereno, said that demand for Hillsborough has soared, with buyers citing a desire for strong schools — and security.

“It’s my first time hearing, ‘Is the glass bulletproof and are there panic rooms?’” Chen said.

Zillow data indicates that home values in Hillsborough, Menlo Park and Piedmont have risen faster over the past year than any other Bay Area city with at least 10,000 people except San Francisco, suggesting that some wealthy buyers are expanding their search radius. In some cities, such as in Piedmont, starter home values—those in the 5th to 35th percentile—have actually climbed more than top-tier values. Still, a “starter home” in Piedmont still costs more than a mid-tier property in San Francisco.

It’s unclear how much demand could rise for homes that aren’t luxury properties or in expensive neighborhoods. One 2019 study found that IPOs increase demand for high-priced homes near the company’s headquarters, but not for low-priced properties. The study’s results also indicated IPOs increased the probability low-income residents moved to lower-income neighborhoods.

But Fairweather said that junior tech workers with smaller windfalls than their senior peers could start to make offers in parts of the Bay Area where their money will go further. Vanessa Hatvany Kitchen, a real estate agent with Compass’ Team Hatvany Kitchen, said her team has seen more people buying condos and smaller single-family homes in San Francisco, fearing they won’t be able to get a foothold after companies like Anthropic go public.

But much of the demand increase is baked in

If there’s any silver lining for buyers, though, it’s that a lot of those companies’ impact on the Bay Area real estate market is already here. Arrian Binnings, a San Francisco real estate agent at Christie’s International Real Estate Sereno, said many AI workers have been able to get cash by borrowing against their stocks or selling their shares via company-approved tender offers, while investors have benefitted from the strength of the public equity market. That’s consistent with some research showing that merely filing for IPOs can raise home prices near a company’s headquarters.

“I don’t see the IPO as the starting gun of a new race. I see it as … we’re probably already at the 50-yard line,” Binnings said. “But I do still see plenty of room to run.”

IPOs don’t always result in an explosion of wealth. In the months after Uber went public in 2019, the company’s stock price plummeted, erasing billions of dollars in value. A similar fate for a public Anthropic or OpenAI would send a chill through the real estate market, some real estate agents say.

But many buyers are optimistic. Chen said some of his clients plan to sell their newly purchased homes in five years, expecting to increase their budgets from $10 million to $25 million. after the IPO. Those buyers, he added, are relatively junior-level employees.

“It’s like Facebook again, but 10x,” Chen said.

The ‘Midlife Crisis’ Isn’t Real. The Actual Risk Is Believing the Myth.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Knowing the reality can benefit your physical and mental health.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

If you ask someone what comes to mind when they hear the word midlife, 99.9 percent of the time the answer will be very specific: crisis. Ideas about middle age tend to skew negative, no matter the cause.

As a midlife researcher, this is worrying to me because I know just how pernicious these legacy beliefs can be. Yet the most prevalent negative interpretations of midlife are not supported by the research. The truth is so much more hopeful and, frankly, interesting.

While it’s hard to predict exactly who will experience this, it’s likely that 10 to 20 percent of people in midlife will go through some intense period of challenge and growth. For those folks, it’s important to remember that this isn’t a bad thing. First of all, the “crisis” is only part of the story, and not even the most interesting part. Whatever challenges you face are likely to be followed by changes to your life that will help you become freer, stronger, and more tuned in to what you really want for yourself in the second half of your life.

Debunking the myth of the midlife crisis isn’t just about correcting the record—it’s about harm reduction. Cultural notions have a real-time effect on our perception of our health and well-being in our middle years, and if the “information” is more myth than reality, that can have negative effects on our physical, psychological, and cognitive health.

Research done by Becca Levy, PhD, a professor of public health and psychology at Yale, and summarized in her book Breaking the Age Code, has shown the power of both negative and positive beliefs about aging to impact our healthspan and lifespan. Levy found that those who had more positive images of aging (e.g., being healthy and active) lived longer than those with the more stereotypical views about older adults as weak, forgetful, and slow. She also found that those with a more positive mindset about aging were healthier, with lower blood pressure and a lower risk of developing Alzheimer’s disease.

Beliefs about aging can eclipse the truth in many arenas. If a woman in her 50s whose children have just left the nest goes to see her physician about feeling excessively fatigued, depressed, and sleepless, the cliché of the midlife crisis makes it more likely that her concerns will be dismissed. This can lead to misdiagnosis for more serious conditions such as depression, autoimmune disorders, or hormonal deficiencies, which if recognized can be treated properly.

One of the best ways to set ourselves up for success when the sea of life starts to get choppy is to pay close attention to the way we are thinking about our story. Whether we know it or not, we are always telling ourselves stories in order to make meaning out of what happens to us and how it makes us feel. When we’re going through a rough patch, it helps immensely to take control of the narrative—moving from a negative frame of mind to a more hopeful one that emphasizes agency and opportunity. On the opposite end of the spectrum from folks who enter midlife ready for battle, many enter midlife on autopilot. Some assume they are stuck with the decisions made early in adulthood. Others want out but don’t imagine the alternatives or can’t see ways to improve circumstances.

Either way, it’s important to remember that it is possible to make tweaks or small changes in midlife; they don’t have to be radical ones. One of my colleagues on the MacArthur Foundation Research Network on Successful Midlife Development used to say, “Some people are so fed up at work that they quit their jobs in frustration, when all they really needed was a two-week vacation.” When we sleepwalk through midlife, at best it’s a missed opportunity for growth, and at worst it simply delays the challenges that will inevitably surface. What we know from the research is that middle age—whether or not you go through a “crisis”—is the time to reflect and make change.

I like to think of midlife as halftime in sports: Some of the important plays have been made, but not all. While a little discomfort with the poor choices of our past isn’t a bad thing, getting mired in regret isn’t something that helps anybody. You have the opportunity to breathe, examine what has come before, and start analyzing what you need to do next to reach your goals or modify them. There is plenty of time left after midlife, so reflection can help people see how they might move in different directions.

For those who don’t know where to begin when it comes to reflecting on their life story and even changing it, there is good research to suggest that a “life review” can help. When done well, a midlife review can help you make sense of the past and bring clarity to your goals moving forward (find out more about how to do it below).

One strategy is to do life reviews at the decade milestones (40, 50, 60) and possibly at the halfway marks (45, 55, 65). Another strategy is to do it when there are significant life events that might require some readjustments, such as going through a divorce, a job loss, or having a child leave home. Midlife is a great time to revisit old stories and even consider some rewrites and new beginnings.

It is important to keep in mind that it is unlikely that you will have accomplished everything you expected to by age 40, 50, or even 60. And it is unrealistic to think you will be happy with all areas of your life. If you think you are having a midlife crisis, ask yourself whether it’s really a crisis or if it’s just that you’re not happy with how some things are going. Maybe it’s just a time to make some changes. The life-shattering, ego-dissembling portrayal of the inescapable midlife crisis we have all become accustomed to is neither inevitable nor universal.

Starlink Mini 2 adds a swappable battery

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The all-in-one satellite dish can now operate for over four hours without an external power source.﻿

The all-in-one satellite dish can now operate for over four hours without an external power source.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

SpaceX announced a new version of its all-in-one satellite internet terminal that integrates a dish, Wi-Fi router, and now a battery into a single device about the size of a laptop. According to some users on X who shared screenshots of invitations to order Starlink Mini 2, the dish is priced at $999, which includes a full year of Roam Unlimited service that SpaceX values at $2,100.

The Starlink Mini 2 will run for up to 4.5 hours on a full charge, SpaceX claims, but that can be expanded by hot-swapping in additional batteries that will be sold separately, or by connecting the dish to a 60W power source over USB-C.

Despite the addition of an integrated battery, the Starlink Mini 2 dish is thinner than the original Mini. SpaceX has managed to slim it down to 23.1mm thick, while the previous version of the Mini was 38.5mm. The Starlink Mini 2 is slightly heavier at around 3.5 pounds as a result of the added battery weight, but carries forward an IP67 dust- and waterproof rating so it’s still safe to use outdoors.

Preorders are now open “by invitation for select US customers,” according to the Starlink website, but “coming soon” to more customers and countries.

All the Brooks Running Shoes and Apparel on Sale Now for Black Friday

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Many of us started running in a Brooks trainer, and with Black Friday deals in full swing, you can stock up on must-have Brooks gear or gift that friend you’re pushing to run their first 5K.

What makes Brooks trainers so popular? Their plush, slipper-like fit and comfy cushioning put them at the center of a Venn diagram where dad shoes and serious runner trainers converge.

Several top-rated models, like the Adrenaline GTS 25, are on sale at brooksrunning.com and sites like REI and Amazon. But sizes and colors are limited, so stock up now before they’re gone for good!

We’ve also included discounted Brooks apparel, so you can run in Brooks from head to toe all winter long.

Brooks Running Sale | REI Brooks Running Sale

The stability counterpart to the Ghost 18, the Adrenaline GTS is the shoe many of us ran in when we fell in love with running. The 25th iteration is the lightest yet, with a foamy midsole, the brand’s DNA Loft v3, to absorb shock and provide soft landings. The guide rails system is Brooks’s unobtrusive stability tech for neutral runners and overpronators alike. Dense foam on the medial and lateral sides acts as bumpers to help align your knee and ankle.

“This is a fantastic shoe,” said tester Chris Garges, an overpronator and heel striker. “I’ve run in every model Adrenaline dating back to the Adrenaline 3, and this is by far the best shoe in the series. I loved everything from the fit to the ride to the cushioning.”

Brooks wanted to change what we should expect when running in a max-cushioned trainer. “We noticed that for some people, they would start running in a neutral shoe and then their body would work extra. So the internal cost was actually increasing,” said Brooks Footwear PLM and BlueLine Director Nikhil Jain.

The team set out to create a shoe that would cut that internal cost. The Glycerin Flex has a decoupled heel and Flex Zone; these components allow your foot to flex naturally instead of working against a stiff, beefy platform.

“Testing the Brooks Glycerin Flex was a novel experience for me on multiple fronts,” said Editorial Assistant Michael McDonough. “Not only was this the first Brooks running shoe I’ve ever run in, but it was also the first hyperflexible shoe that I have ever seen—and overall, it was a positive experience. The Glycerin Flex combines the benefits of a daily trainer that allows your feet to work and get stronger with the comfort and protection of a maximalist shoe.”

We like the Glycerin Max 2 for run/walking and recovery days. The shoe has 45 mm of DNA Tuned foam in the heel and a 6 mm offset. Its beefiness was off-putting for some, especially for runners hoping to get in some tempo work on their long runs. But testers said the cushioning was extremely comfy on slow days.

“I loved how comfortable the sneaker was. I instantly felt supported and it felt like I was walking on a bouncy pillow,” said wear-tester Steph Norman. “This sneaker would be great for easy/long miles where you do not have to worry about your feet hurting.”

The popular Brooks Ghost hits the trails. The shoe is outfitted for going off-road with 3 mm lugs on the TrailTack rubber outsole.

Our tester, David Hinkl, appreciated how the shoe protected his feet on gnarly surfaces. “The best part of the shoe for me was the sole,” he said. “It was beefy enough to prevent sharp rocks and pointy things from getting to my feet. Training is more fun when I don’t have to tiptoe across the trail.”

The Canopy is iconic. Despite the curtains of rain, monstrous winds, and cold temps, Des Linden was the first American woman to win the 2018 Boston Marathon after 33 years. And she wore the Canopy Jacket. This version of the Canopy (the original is also on sale with 30 percent off) can be packed into a little pouch that’s easy to store in the depths of an overpacked duffel.

The High Point keeps out the rain without overheating. Venting in the back allows airflow, while waterproof fabric and sealed inner seams keep you from getting soaked during a downpour. The jacket also packs down into a pocket.

These joggers are the softest joggers we’ve tested. They’re great for warmups, cooldowns, and traveling. I should know—I trekked Japan after running the Tokyo Marathon in these.

The Run Visible not only ensures you’re seen with reflective detail, but it also keeps you warm thanks to Thermolite insulation. Three pockets provide storage. That hi-vis orange color also helps you pop out in low light.

The Chaser is for runners who love a soft compression short with ample storage. The high-rise Chaser has side pockets for stashing your phone and leg-opening grippers to prevent them from riding up.

I want an uglier GTA 6 that runs at 60 FPS – 30 FPS on console is more reason to wait for PC

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Opinion | Please, Rockstar, join us in the light of 60 FPS

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 6 is targeting 30 FPS on PS5 and Xbox Series X. The same goes for the beefier PS5 Pro, which can now sit another exclusive on the shelf alongside Sony's marketing deal for the game: ray-traced shadows that have seen some people shell out $1,300. This is all fine and expected news, yet I'm saddled by the same tiny disappointment I feel every time a big console release targets 30 FPS.

It's not that I'll turn my nose at any game that isn't running at 60 FPS or higher, filthy PC gamer that I am. But if we're talking preferences, yeah, I do wish everything ran at 60 FPS at 1440p or below. I'd gladly accept lower graphical fidelity as a tradeoff for that and, more pertinently, I'll happily wait to play GTA 6 on PC if it means better performance.

This is easy for me to say, of course. I didn't have to move mountains to get a game of this scale running at 30 FPS to begin with. It might sound easy to put fidelity and frame rate on a scale and just subtract material until the FPS side rises, but I know it's not that simple, even if cuts could surely be made.

It's not just about the visible sweat, intricate clothing, innumerable environmental wrinkles, granular animations, or ray-traced shadows – all the little details that people at Rockstar have presumably achieved through blood sacrifice. This might actually be the best-looking game ever made by many metrics. But GTA 6 also has to handle dense NPC crowds, a sprawling city, seamless character switching, and all the unseen systems supporting that experience. That, to me, is the truly important, gameplay-affecting stuff.

I never care about graphics in any way that matters, and I always care about smoothness

It all amounts to more things for that frame rate to haul, and Rockstar seemingly chose 30 FPS with all the fixins' over higher FPS with all the sacrifices. I understand and respect that. Devs should make what they want, as ludicrously complex as they want it to be. But on that scale, my taste leans heavily toward frame rate for a few reasons.

Console generational leaps are getting smaller. This generation has, as far as I'm concerned, had only two real difference-makers: M.2 NVMe SSDs that dramatically improve loading times, and the spread of performance-focused graphic settings (or performance modes). As you may have guessed, I go for performance mode every single time.

Some games benefit more from having higher frame rates than others, but maximum FPS is always my pick until I hit 60. A fast-paced martial arts game like Phantom Blade Zero would be seriously hurt by a low or inconsistent frame rate, for instance. But I'm currently playing Persona 3 Reload, and its turn-based combat and simple environments aren't really bothered as long as the frame rate is half-decent and stable. But the choice is still simple: I never care about graphics in any way that matters, and I always care about smoothness. GTA 6 isn't a frenetic wuxia fiesta, but its driving and shooting definitely push it toward the more FPS-sensitive camp in my mind.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

There's a point where I lose interest in graphical improvements, and that point came at least 10 years ago. I can appreciate a cutting-edge game, whether it's GTA 6 NPCs whose calves flex with each step or a huge RPG like Cyberpunk 2077 with every setting cranked to the moon. But if AAA games eternally looked like late-gen PS4 releases, I honestly wouldn't complain. My threshold for game that looks good is comfortably below that, and an art style far removed from realism is more likely to grab me anyway. Now and forever, I'm more interested in games that run better and console gaming that feels, not necessarily looks, more like my PC experience (again, M.2s really helped with this). Hell, right now I just want consoles that people can actually afford, and pushing hardware to fruitlessly advance graphics won't get us those.

Every generation, I think these will surely be the systems to normalize 60 FPS, and I'm consistently let down by games going in the opposite direction for visuals that don't, and will never, move me. In all graphical discussions, my mind goes to an early Uncharted: Drake's Fortune demo showing how water would convincingly dampen Nathan Drake's clothes and then dry in the sun. Nearly 20 years later, it feels like we are still hung up on the equivalent of Nathan Drake getting wet even though this detail didn't mean squat to the experience of Uncharted and this kind of thing is infinitely less novel and relevant today. The real or imagined title of 'best-looking game ever made' has never meant less, and a significantly uglier version of GTA 6 would still assuredly blow my socks off. I'll tell you what would really impress me: a promise that this runs at twice the frame rate of GTA 5, a 13-year-old game.

He's been with GamesRadar+ since 2019. They've yet to realize his position is a cover for his career-spanning Destiny column, and he's kept the ruse going with a lot of news and the occasional feature, all while playing as many roguelikes as possible.

The 12 Best Amber Colognes to Buy Right Now

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are the season’s top cold-weather compliment getters.

It has been an unusually warm fall so far, but today, at least in New York City, there’s finally a chill in the air. I got so excited when I saw the temperature was in the low 50s this morning that I may have rushed the season a tad and put on a thick sweater under an insulated jacket. So, I was sweating in the subway, but at least I smelled good—because when it comes to cold-weather fragrance, I know what I’m doing.

If you’re looking to add to your winter cologne rotation, there’s no better place to start than a good amber scent. When you think of fragrance as an accessory, it becomes something you strive to match with your outfit, and amber colognes pair perfectly with corduroy, thick coats, and soft scarves. But what exactly does amber smell like? After all, the kind of amber that probably comes to mind—fossilized tree amber—doesn’t actually have much of a scent.

Well, when we say “amber” in the fragrance world, it really refers to a blend of natural resins and synthetics that combine to produce a warm, rich, and sweet smell. Some classic scent notes that make up the amber fragrance family include labdanum (a resinous extract from a Mediterranean shrub), benzoin (a sweet, balsamic tree resin), ambroxan (a synthetic fragrance molecule with a warm, woody aroma), vanilla, and tonka bean.

Here, we’ve pulled together our definitive list of the top amber scents for men, covering every vibe, lifestyle, and scent story you’re trying to tell. Whether you’re leaning into smoky sophistication, something on the sweeter side, or going for “I naturally smell this good,” there’s something here for everyone. And with the holidays fast approaching, consider these foolproof gift options for anyone on your list who appreciates smelling like they have their life together.

Inspired by the luxury fashion label’s most iconic men’s silhouette, this fragrance is designed to capture the essence of a tuxedo, using notes that convey sophistication and sharp black-tie tailoring into scent. Grounded in earthy patchouli and layered with subtle spice, it radiates a refined, confident energy that’s perfect for winter yet versatile enough for wearing it year-round.

The purpose of Maison Margiela’s Replica line is that the scents are made to capture memories and moods, the scent of a specific moment. By the Fireplace is easily one of the most evocative scents in the collection—my personal favorite, for what it’s worth—because smoke and warm chestnut instantly transport you somewhere near a warm crackling fire.

It starts with a bit of spice and smoke, and once it settles on the skin, that initial smokiness softens into a smooth, vanilla warmth. The rest of the fragrance leans very heavily into the sweetness of amber. It’s a compliment-getter, if that’s what you’re after.

If you want to channel your inner Alexander Skarsgård in Pillion (IYKYK) with a scent that radiates rugged confidence, subtle earthiness, and that intoxicating new-leather smell that only comes from a new pair of boots or a leather trench, look no further. Malin + Goetz’s Leather Eau de Parfum has been a staple in my collection for years

It’s leathery and musky, but it has a good hit of cedar that combines to leans it into the world of amber. It also has a lot of floral notes as it dries on the skin to balance it all out. It’s a nicely layered fragrance. A purist might not call this amber, but we think it’s well within the sweet, resinous world of these other fragrances.

Not ready to commit to the full bottle? The unisex cologne also comes as a perfume oil in a compact rollerball perfect for a trial run or as a stocking stuffer.

As I noted in my intro, amber fragrances can take on a variety of scent profiles, all capturing the warmth and depth of true amber. Rich gourmand options like Horace’s Vintage Vanilla are a great pick if you love scents inspired by the culinary world, but don’t worry, it’s expertly balanced with a mix of woodiness and spice so you won’t end up smelling like you went overboard on your marshmallow-y Victoria’s Secret body mist.

Don’t even want to smell like an edible vanilla confection? No problem. The brand’s signature Oud Rose is another solid amber-forward scent that leans on floral notes to balance its intensity.

Diptyque is another one of those bougie fragrance houses everyone recognizes, yet for some reason I rarely reach for their scents over my tried-and-true favorites. But after smelling L’Eau Trois Eau de Toilette, I might have to rethink that stance. This one immediately transported me to being in Italy and stepping into a Duomo, where incense and myrrh linger in the air.

It takes a surprisingly verdant and green approach to the amber family, but those resinous top notes anchor everything with a spicy-smoky warmth. The result is a scent that feels refined, atmospheric, and just mysterious enough to keep you coming back for another sniff.

Sure, amber scents can be sweet and gourmand, but they can also be spicy and sophisticated. Giardini di Toscana’s Bianco Oro is a great example of the latter. At first spritz, you mostly get spicy pink and black pepper with a backdrop of amber, but as the scent lingers, the rich amber notes take center stage along with smooth musks and creamy woods. As it fades, it becomes a bit powdery, but the trio of spices, amber, and musks are still there. It’s the perfect scent to wear out to a nice dinner or evening event, but it works just as well in the daytime.

It’s a general rule that amber and woods go great together, since amber provides a warm richness that blends well with dry, crisp woods. That combination is at the foundation of Dolce & Gabbana’s The One for Men EDP, but before we get there, we actually get a touch of bright and juicy citrus from Tarocco orange and grapefruit as well as a natural, herbal hint of clary sage at the heart that adds a bit of earthiness to this fragrance. Amber and woods mingle at the base, grounding the lighter, more vibrant opening in something warm and rich.

Guerlain makes a handful of luxury amber fragrances, but Tobacco Honey is the one that stands out from the rest. It’s an intense blend of—you guessed it—tobacco leaves and golden honey, which creates a beautifully bold contrast of smoky and sweet. It emphasizes the sweet side of the ambery tobacco accord at the heart of this scent, enveloping the dry tobacco notes in vanilla, tonka bean, and sesame to create a rich, creamy heart that gets lifted up by vibrant, sweet honey. If you’ve ever wanted to try a tobacco fragrance but worried it would smell too smoky (or too much like cigarettes), this is the one that will change your mind.

For something a bit lighter and more airy than the mostly rich and deep amber scents on this list, try Golden Morning from Lafco’s new (and first-ever) personal fragrance collection. Like a cup of hot chamomile tea with honey, it’s soothing, warm, and a little sweet. Bright and citrusy at the top with bergamot and apple blossom, it then introduces chamomile and a floral heart of lavender, violet leaf, and rose. The dry down is smooth and sweet with honey, tonka bean, amber, and musks.

Okay, I’m not saying the other fragrances on this list can’t also be considered “bougie,” but Maison Francis Kurkdjian Paris’ Baccarat Rouge 540 takes luxury to an entirely new level. And yes, while the price tag is particularly eye-watering, I have to admit: this scent is absolutely worth it. A perfectly balanced blend of woody, floral, and ambery notes, this fragrance uses high-quality ingredients with a higher molecular weight, meaning they cling to the skin longer and create a richer scent profile with impressive staying power. In short, a little goes a very long way with this one…if that helps you justify the price tag.

Sitting somewhere between gourmand and citrus on the fragrance spectrum, Arquiste’s L’Or de Louis is one of those amber scents that just radiates richness. The 24K gold flakes are a little too on-the-nose, but the the story behind the fragrance does a better job of telling a story of opulence. It was inspired by the moments when King Louis XIV and his court strolled Versailles’ Grand Orangery, enjoying the flowering orange trees warmed by bonfires.

It also won The Fragrance Foundation’s Indie Fragrance of the Year Award in 2024, so the hoity-toity storytelling is backed by a scent that actually smells…well…good. The result is a golden aura of perfectly blended smokiness, fruitiness, and depth. A fragrance truly fit for a king.

If your nose is on the more sensitive side and you’re looking for a warm, enveloping scent that doesn’t overwhelm with heavy muskiness, D’Orsay’s Te dire oui. V.H. Eau de Parfum is a perfect choice. Built on leather, it avoids the overpowering muskiness found in many amber and leather fragrances, opting instead for a cleaner, sportier interpretation rounded out by a subtle saffron and rose finish.

This aligns perfectly with the brand’s philosophy, which favors fragrances that act as personal rituals—subtle companions that are seamlessly woven into everyday life—rather than scents that dominate every room you enter.

Expert Tips for Scouting, Scattering, and Tagging Fall Turkeys

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Fall turkey hunting requires matching your strategy to the specific flock type you're pursuing. Adult gobblers, hen-poult family groups, and second-year jakes all behave and vocalize differently. The spring yelp that pulled in a gobbler won't work in October. Identify the birds first, then dial in your calls and approach accordingly.

Turkeys typically only receive passing mentions when folks discuss their favorite fall pursuits. And with spring being a distant memory and other seasons in full swing, that’s understandable. And unfortunate. Fall turkeys offer a sporting experience unlike any other outdoors adventure. And with more than 40 states offering autumn turkey seasons—some of which open in September and others that run deep into winter—opportunities abound.

But the fall game plays out somewhat differently than the familiar spring ritual. Here’s a quick look at the types of turkeys you’ll likely encounter in the autumn woods and some tips on how to bring one home.

Many hunters kill fall turkeys incidentally while pursuing deer or small game, sometimes encountering flocks at harvested crop fields or mast-rich wooded areas. There’s nothing wrong with that, but true students of the autumn game dig far deeper into turkey behavior and learn to interact with each type of fall bird.

During fall, turkeys are in different modes than in spring, as they’re no longer concerned with breeding. Food and the struggle for survival dominate their existence. They typically fall into several distinct flock types: adult gobblers, hens without poults, and hens with broods of poults. Several subcategories also exist: Male birds in their second autumn—those that were jakes the previous spring—group together. These are sometimes called jake-and-a-halfs. Also, hen-poult groups often combine into larger family flocks, called crèches. The various groups sometimes intermingle, especially at prime feeding areas or large common roost sites. Often, however, you’ll encounter them separately.

As with spring, calling can be critical in autumn for finding turkeys and getting them close. However, turkeys—especially hens and poults—can be much more vocal in fall, and their lexicon expands to include sounds many spring hunters don’t hear much. Roost sites that attract family flocks can sound like a din of turkey chatter at dawn. Young turkeys often utter kee-kees (a series of three to four ascending whistles) and kee-kee runs, which are whistling series followed by a crude yelp or two. The vocalizations might sound slightly different depending on whether it’s uttered by a jake or jenny. Brood hens use long, pleading strings of assembly yelps to gather their offspring after being separated. Gobblers are typically less vocal in fall, communicating via slow, raspy gobbler yelps and popping clucks. Still, it’s not uncommon to hear them gobble at times.

Because the various types of turkeys act and vocalize differently in fall, general hunting approaches can fall flat. For example, the straightforward hen yelps that made a gobbler respond in April will likely be met with indifference from male turkeys in October. To boost your chances, you must know the kind of bird you’re hunting and then fine-tune your strategy.

Generally, fall hunts fall under two broad categories: 1) Hunters who call in an attempt to make turkeys come to them. 2) Hunters, or specially trained turkey dogs, who scatter groups of turkeys, then set up and try to call the birds back. Knowing the type of turkey you’re after is especially critical during scatter scenarios.

“You need to be the turkey that calls to you,” said Marlin Watkins, a noted Ohio call maker and expert fall turkey hunter. “A young gobbler is not coming to hen sounds. You need to be that bird, whether it’s a jenny hen, young gobbler, or adult hen. And if you produce those sounds, you will be far more successful.”

When his dogs scatter a flock, Watkins immediately tries to determine the group’s composition—hens, gobblers, or a family flock—as that will dictate his calling approach. “If I’ve scattered turkeys, I start with jenny hen kees and kee-kee runs, provided it’s a family flock,” he said. “As soon as first bird answers, I mimic that bird. A young gobbler is not coming to a jenny hen normally, so if a young gobbler kees and gobbler yelps, that’s what I do, even to the point of going note for note. As the bird approaches, I soften the calling, but I do not quit, or they will walk away. They can hear other birds calling that I can’t.”

Depending on the flock type, reassembling turkeys might present a shot opportunity within 20 or 30 minutes. However, adult hens and toms often take longer to get back together, so Watkins uses a different approach after flushing gangs of gobblers or jake-and-a-halfs. “We usually wait 30 minutes or more and start with gobbler yelping and clucking,” he said. “If we get no response in an hour or more, we will mix in gobbling. We start slow, only making a call or two every 10 or more minutes. After no response for that hour or more, we get a little more aggressive.”

That patient yet observant mindset can pay off, as it’s often unclear how birds will react. “I’ve broken gobblers one day and sat three hours, heard one cluck and then shot him,” Watkins said. “The very next day, I’ve broken a different flock, and they started gobbling in an hour and gobbled nonstop until we killed one. You just never know what they will do.”

When hunting alone, or in situations when you can’t scatter turkey flocks, calling strategies become more opportunistic. Brett Ladeau, a Vermont hunter and contest caller, often starts fall quests with a basic tactic. “I typically approach a fall hunt by finding roost sites and try to pattern either a family flock or mixed flock,” he said. “To locate birds, I scout much like I do in spring. I like to be close to birds while they are still on the roost, so finding roost areas is an important tactic I use to find fall birds. I scout for roosting areas and mark them on my mapping app for future hunts. If possible, I also like to watch or hear birds fly to the roost in the evening so I know where they will be in the morning.”

If a roost hunt doesn’t pan out, Ladeau tries to locate turkeys via calling, often taking a mobile approach. “I use my spring technique of riding from spot to spot where I know there are birds, and once I find a few spots that hold birds, I will cold-call at a few well-known turkey haunts,” he said.

And as with hunting scattered groups, Ladeau tries to match his calling to the vocalizations of responsive birds. “If I’m hunting a family group or mixed group, I will use the kee-kee a lot, along with assembly yelps and other calls,” he said. “If I’m hunting a mixed flock or a group of male birds, I will gobble- and jake-yelp to try to entice them into range. My setup is to try to get as close to the birds as possible and then start my calling sequence. If I’m close to a roost, I might not call much, especially if I know their routine, but if not, I call pretty aggressively.”

Get to know the flocks of autumn this year, and fine-tune your calling to bring them into shooting range. Ducks and big bucks can wait a bit as you enjoy the sights and sounds of the fall turkey woods.
