Stem Cells Recreate the Human Placenta’s Hidden First Steps

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Biomedical engineers and developmental biologists at Columbia University have engineered 3D human stem cell models that recreate the earliest, previously inaccessible stages of human placental formation. Published in Nature Cell Biology, the research led by Dr. Mijo Simunovic reveals how human pluripotent stem cells, temporarily reset to a naive state, can be biochemically steered to suppress Hippo signaling, initiating the embryo’s first major cell-fate decision.

The cells self-organize over three weeks into complex chorionic tissue architectures with fluid-filled lacunae that mirror early implantation, unlocking a mechanistic platform to investigate early pregnancy failure, preeclampsia, and fetal growth restriction.

Source: Columbia University / Nature Cell Biology

The placenta is the transient, life-sustaining organ that anchors mammalian pregnancy, acting as the fetus’s lungs, kidneys, liver, and immune barrier. Despite its fundamental role in human reproduction, the earliest days of placental formation have remained an impenetrable biological black box.

Because early implantation occurs deep within the human endometrium shortly after conception, it cannot be optically tracked or ethically biopsied in vivo. Furthermore, comparative animal studies offer limited insight; the molecular and physical dynamics of human blastocyst implantation diverge sharply from those of standard laboratory rodent models.

To overcome this longstanding barrier, a team led by Dr. Mijo Simunovic at Columbia University has developed a functional three-dimensional, lab-grown stem cell model that successfully recapitulates the earliest stages of human placenta formation.

Published in Nature Cell Biology, the work provides an experimental window into how pregnancy begins and why it so frequently terminates in early miscarriage.

Within days of fertilization, the human blastocyst faces its very first developmental crossroad. Cells on the outer perimeter must separate from the inner cell mass (which forms the embryo proper) and commit to an extraembryonic fate.

These outer cells establish the chorion, the foundational tissue that invades maternal uterine tissue to assemble the functional placenta. Molecular errors during this crucial transition can cause implantation failure, recurrent pregnancy loss, or devastating mid-to-late gestational pathologies, including preeclampsia and intrauterine fetal growth restriction (FGR).

To model this transition in vitro, Simunovic’s laboratory, bridging Columbia’s Department of Chemical Engineering and Department of Genetics and Development, focused on the Hippo signaling pathway. Known primarily for regulating organ size, contact inhibition, and mechanotransduction, Hippo signaling acts as the primary molecular compass governing this initial divergence.

The researchers initiated their platform using human pluripotent stem cells:

“What is remarkable is that we don’t have to tell each cell what to become or where to go,” says Simunovic. “Once we set the developmental process in motion, the cells begin to organize themselves into a complex tissue. We can now study how that happens.”

Over a 21-day timeline, the stem cell cultures transitioned from flat cultures into complex 3D organotypic structures.

The cells differentiated into the principal lineages of the early human chorion and spontaneously migrated into anatomical layers that closely resemble in vivo placental architecture.

Remarkably, the 3D structures formed internal, fluid-filled cavities identical to placental lacunae—specialized microscopic chambers that emerge during human implantation to capture and interface with invading maternal spiral arteries.

“Being able to watch a human placenta form in the lab should be a boon to both basic embryology and clinical research,” explains Simunovic. “We hope it will be useful in studying how the placenta forms and how its formation can go awry, which is one of the leading causes of early pregnancy failure.”

Because human implantation is distinct from other mammals, this platform enables researchers to test human-specific hypotheses regarding mechanical forces, endocrine signals, and cellular cross-talk at the maternal-fetal boundary.

Future iterations of the model will co-culture these placental structures with endometrial organoids, allowing scientists to simulate the complete implantation sequence in vitro. By identifying the molecular checkpoints where placental formation fails, the system provides a preclinical engine to uncover early diagnostic biomarkers and targeted therapeutics to treat infertility, recurrent miscarriage, and pregnancy-induced hypertension.

Why has early human placenta formation been so difficult to study?

Early placental formation occurs deep inside the uterus days after fertilization, making direct observation impossible without terminating pregnancy. Furthermore, human embryo implantation is biologically and architecturally distinct from rodents, rendering traditional animal models inadequate for studying human-specific defects.

How did the Columbia researchers trigger placental differentiation from stem cells?

The researchers temporarily reset human pluripotent stem cells to a naive state of expanded potency and then applied targeted biochemical signals to suppress the Hippo signaling pathway. This suppression mirrored the natural developmental switch that directs the embryo’s outer cells to form the chorion.

What structures did the lab-grown placental models develop?

Over three weeks, the cells spontaneously differentiated into the major chorionic lineages and organized into distinct 3D layers. They also developed fluid-filled cavities resembling placental lacunae, the early anatomical structures that connect with maternal blood vessels during uterine implantation.

Reconstructing the emergence of the human chorion via Hippo-mediated trophoblast induction

Differential Hippo–LATS–YAP signalling controls the first mammalian cell-fate decision, yet how human trophectoderm specification progresses to chorion formation remains unknown. Here we establish a developmentally informed model in which LATS kinase inhibition initiates the trophectoderm trajectory without exogenous morphogens.

Stage matching to an integrated embryo–chorion single-cell reference revealed ordered progression from early trophectoderm to secondary chorionic lineages. Whole-genome bisulfite sequencing showed a naive-like hypomethylated state followed by remethylation, with key known placenta-associated imprinted domains retaining methylation and allelic bias. We then established a three-dimensional human chorion model by agitating induced-cell aggregates in suspension, yielding secondary chorionic lineages that self-organized with outward-facing trophoblast polarity and inside-out stromal–epithelial topology.

Endogenous WNT signalling was dispensable for initial trophectoderm induction but regulated later chorionic lineage balance. Isolating CLDN6+ITGA6− cells identified a proEVT-like intermediate with villous and extravillous potential.

Together, this framework connects human trophectoderm induction to three-dimensional chorion modelling and enables investigation of placental specification.

Google Expands Ads In AI Mode To 10+ Countries, Adds Multiturn Ads via @sejournal, @brookeosmundson

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Google is expanding ads in AI Mode to 10+ countries in English and launching multiturn ads in the U.S. Here is what PPC advertisers need to know about reporting.

Google is expanding ads in AI Mode to more than 10 new countries in English while launching multiturn ads for follow-up queries in the U.S.

Google shared the update from its official Google Ads X account on November 9, following an earlier LinkedIn post from Dan Taylor noting plans to bring ads in AI Mode to additional countries in Q4.

Eligible campaigns can already participate, though advertisers still can’t see AI Mode performance separately in Google Ads.

Here’s what’s changing, how conversational placements work, and what you need to know when clients ask about AI Mode reporting.

Google’s October announcement named 10 additional countries where ads in AI Mode are expanding:

The expansion currently supports English. Google also announced plans to bring ads in AI Mode to Brazil, Mexico, and the United Kingdom in Q4 2026. Those markets were listed as upcoming rather than part of the initial rollout.

Google’s availability documentation notes that Pakistan supports text ads only.

Multiturn ads are launching in the U.S., allowing ads to appear on follow-up questions within an AI Mode conversation. Google hasn’t announced when those placements will expand outside the U.S.

Google describes AI Mode as a search experience for questions involving exploration, reasoning, or complex comparisons. Users can ask a detailed question an dcontinue with follow-ups as they work through their options.

AI Mode may use “query fan-out,” issuing related searches across subtopics and data sources to develop a response.

With multiturn ads, Google can show an ad during those follow-up exchanges when the conversation has commercial relevance.

According to Google’s advertiser documentation, ads can match directly to a search query or draw on context from both the question and AI response. An exploratory question can then create an advertising opportunity even when the user hasn’t searched for a specific product.

In his LinkedIn update, Taylor noted that people are asking longer, more detailed, and exploratory questions in AI Mode, and described many of those interactions as upper-funnel queries that carry commercial intent.

For advertisers, that means ads may reach people still comparing approaches or deciding what they need. A longer question or another follow-up does not, on its own, indicate that someone is ready to buy.

As ads in AI Mode expand, clients will likely ask whether their campaigns are appearing there and how those ads perform.

In my view, the most important thing for agency and in-house teams to communicate right now is that reporting is limited, and you can’t separate out AI Mode performance granularly.

Google’s reporting documentation confirms that AI Mode impressions, clicks, conversions, and costs are included in standard campaign reporting. Search terms are also combined with other traffic, without a separate AI Mode breakdown. That prevents advertisers from calculating an AI Mode-specific CPA or ROAS.

Google’s keyword prioritization documentation also says reported search terms for experiences including AI Mode may approximate the user’s intent. Advertisers should not assume they are seeing the exact question someone entered.

Teams can still review those terms for relevance, compare conversion performance, and check lead quality. If results change after the rollout, though, the timing alone can’t directly establish that AI Mode caused it.

When clients ask how AI Mode ads are performing, advertisers can explain that those results are included in campaign totals. Google Ads doesn’t report them separately, so teams can’t show how much spend or how many conversions came from AI Mode.

Google is adding countries and follow-up placements without giving advertisers a separate view of their results.

That leaves teams answering questions about AI Mode with campaign-level data. A campaign could improve overall while AI Mode underperforms, or the reverse, and the combined totals wouldn’t show that granularity.

Separate reporting would help advertisers understand which searches and placements are producing sales or qualified leads. Until Google provides it, teams can explain how their campaigns qualify for AI Mode, but can’t necessarily tell clients what those placements contributed to.

Now Yourself: The Elusive Science of What the Present Moment Is Made of and How It Makes You Who You Are

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

“Whatever has happened, whatever is going to happen in the world, it is the living moment that contains the sum of the excitement, this moment in which we touch life and all the energy of the past and future,” Muriel Rukeyser wrote in one of the greatest books of all time.

“Fearlessness is what love seeks [which] exists only in the complete calm that can no longer be shaken by events expected of the future,” Hannah Arendt wrote in another of them, “hence the only valid tense is the present, the Now.”

But upon closer inspection, now — this elementary particle of eternity, this tiny and total locus of the living moment, this constant that is never the same — turns out to be more elusive than a neutrino, passing through us ghostly and ungraspable, yet leaving in its wake the purest sum of what we are.

Like love, now is an entirely subjective experience built on a meaningful interaction between systems. Like love, it is not a state but a process — a dynamic creation that enlists all of our past experience and the entire pattern of predictive perceptions we call reality. Like love, it is more like music than like mathematics.

Jo Marchant takes up this elemental mystery in her excellent investigation In Search of Now: The Science of the Present Moment (public library), weaving together physics, psychology, neuroscience, philosophy, and cultural anthropology to expose the warp and weft of our aliveness, locating in the living now “the origins of both life and mind, the driving force that powers behaviours, perceptions, choices and decisions, that ultimately carves out self and time.”

It isn’t a location within time at all, but what makes time possible. Now is nature itself: the experienced, evolving universe within which all time, and all life, is held.

Two centuries after the vitalism debate sundered science into warring camps over the search for a “vital spark” that makes matter alive, we are finding that conscious minds — that crowning achievement of matter — are made of time and bodies undone by it, that it is the fundamental substrate of our aliveness. If the moment is the vital spark of time, the science of now — divisive, thrilling, inconclusive — is the vitalism debate of our time.

It began when Einstein defeated Bergson in their historic debate. Relativity rendered the flow of time, and the immediacy of the moment nested within it, not a given of physical reality but a function of the vantage you take. “The baggage of consciousness,” Einstein himself called our sense of time in a letter to his best friend. Like all radical ideas, relativity sent the ideological pendulum in the opposite direction and the ancients’ notion of eternalism — the idea that time is absolute, the same in all directions, and all existence simply is, without dynamic being that flows from past to present to future — was revived in the modern model of the block universe, configuring spacetime as an unchanging four-dimensional block. Marchant describes the implications of that model:

Our lives aren’t unfurling plots or stories; they are intricate paths already mapped out in four dimensions… Every cell within your body — your neurons, muscle cells, the blood cells pulsing through your arteries, capillaries and veins — has its own intricate, interconnecting life path carved out through the block. And not just every cell, but every atom. Each of us is made up of trillions of strands in space-time, all with their own complex trajectory. Your whole life might look like a sort of tree carved into the block, with disparate strands coming together at one end, representing your conception and birth; gradually thickening into a trunk; and then at the other end splaying out into finer and finer branches before disintegrating completely at the point of your death and decomposition… There is no room for movement, flow or happening. Reality doesn’t become. It just is.

If the physicists are right, our attachment to the specialness of the present moment is just another example of how our limited perception deceives us, like thinking the sky turns or the Earth is flat.

Causality, this model implies, is simply an interpretation based on our limited perception: “The flow from past to future… rather than being a fundamental feature of the universe… emerges as a secondary consequence of our inability to see the full picture.”

Then there is the predictive coding model, under which “what we perceive — the vibrant, changing, three-dimensional reality all around us — isn’t the external world at all, but a guided prediction, or as some have described it, a ‘controlled hallucination’… a prediction built from our history, both recent impressions and a lifetime of experience.”

It is worth remembering here that what gives science its loveliness and potency, and what distinguishes it from philosophy, is the passion for building models of how nature works calibrated by the rigor of testing them against reality. And yet time may be the only region where the models are truly and fundamentally untestable because the modeler is a captive of time. Einstein’s equations gave us the mathematical foundation for the Big Bang, but not even Einstein could travel back to the beginning of time to see if the model was true. This may be why, to me, the most compelling — as well as the most poetic — portion of Merchant’s investigation is the most empirical: an fMRI study that analyzed the patterns of brain activity in people watching a movie, which has a built-in timeline, or simply resting, capturing one image per second and comparing how these images — these portraits of the moment — differ from one another in order to render the experience of time’s passage. Marchant details the astonishing revelation:

There isn’t a simple progression from one brain state to the next as time passes, with each moment most similar to its nearest neighbour. Our “brain patterns are not simply counting off the seconds,” says [study author Dan] Lloyd. Hidden within the sequences was an organised temporal structure, with regular patterns in the way that subjects’ brains moved back and forth between a small number of states. In fact, the structure he found is very like that of music. Lloyd identified short, repeating motifs, or themes: sequences of states, between 4 and 11 seconds long, that look similar to each other. Often these themes recurred at constant time intervals: he called that “rhythm.” These rhythms appeared at a range of different timescales, and sometimes these frequencies were related to each other, so that they nested within one another perfectly. Lloyd called this structure “harmony” because it is analogous to the harmonic vibrations that give musical instruments, from violins to saxophones, their rich, resonant sound.

What this “harmony” means is that at any single moment, our spontaneous brain activity is made up of multiple, overlaid patterns and rhythms, which are related yet change over different timescales: just like our experience of Now. Each moment of neural activity is influenced by what’s happened in both the near and further past, and in turn influences what will happen in the near and further future. The results “suggest a human capacity to spread out from the immediate present tense of sensation, towards an overall temporal landscape,” Lloyd concludes. This explains how we can navigate fast-changing events yet at the same time hold on to stable threads of where we’re going and who we are.

An epoch before neuroimaging, Virginia Woolf intuited this truth when she considered the “moments of being” that make us who we are, intuited the musicality of being alive: “The whole world is a work of art [and] we are parts of the work of art,” she wrote in her breathtaking epiphany in the middle of the garden, “Hamlet or a Beethoven quartet is the truth about this vast mass that we call the world. But there is no Shakespeare, there is no Beethoven; certainly and emphatically there is no God; we are the words; we are the music; we are the thing itself.”

Looking through the kaleidoscope of the various models, Marchant considers the essence of the light:

It seems our perception of Now is a combination of two crucial factors: the ability to bind a hierarchy of different timescales together within each moment; and the inexorable progression from one moment to the next. This highly ordered temporal composition underpins our flowing stream of consciousness. Passing time is not just a characteristic we perceive: it is the underlying frame or structure through which we experience reality.

And yet it may be more important even than that, underpinning not just our world but who we are.

Our lives, Marchant argues, are only really alive, only ever real, as the moment lives itself through us:

The perceptions and sensations themselves — the call and response, the meeting or thwarting of predictions — these are reality. These are what existence is made of… Our perceptions or experiences — the melancholy of raindrops on a window, the exhilaration of diving into an icy pool — are real in themselves. There is no separate, enduring landscape beyond that they’re based on, no solid reference point against which our sensations can be judged.

Now has objective meaning as the expanding boundary at which reality is continually created. What’s coming into being includes not just the contents of the universe but its very structure. As new events occur, new universe — new space-time — is being born.

With an eye to all the different models of physics she examines in the book — relativity, the block universe, enactivism, and predictive coding among them — she ends where we ought to always begin: the discipline of not mistaking the model for the thing itself:

Do we exist as frozen snapshots or mathematical braids? Are we logic-bound computers or dynamic hurricanes? Are we living in a mental realm of shadows, separated from true reality by impenetrable, iron-like walls? Or are our perceptions real, while the familiar things of our world — even time and space themselves — are mere statistical structures, predictions that help us to manage our flow of sensations and stay alive?… Perhaps with all these possibilities there’s no way even to approach what lies beyond us, beyond our senses, beyond this point in time… All any of us can ever really know is that this moment exists. Maybe that’s enough. What we’re sensing and feeling, right here, right now, is real and undeniable, precisely because we are experiencing it.

Because there is no commons of now, the moment is the measure of our loneliness in time, but also the only region of space where we flower into being fully ourselves in a constant bloom of becoming.

What we perceive or experience in any moment is so personal, so utterly bound up in our individual history and biology, that it doesn’t make sense to speak of any “true,” definitive way of things outside that process… Our inner worlds — from feeling ownership of our bodies to experiencing emotions or recalling our life stories — are complex webs of probabilistic inferences, ever-changing depending on our circumstances, and recreated in each moment. There are no separate, enduring “selves” sitting behind… [We] exist as dynamic, living patterns of personal experiences, not stand-alone things. There’s no external stage on which we’re acting, no pre-existing terrain into which we’ve been parachuted. And, on the other side of the coin, there’s no pre-existing “us” either: no floating essences or souls ready to cast their gaze on the world.

This is not a negation of our being but an affirmation of it — a liberation from the tyranny and tedium of selfing we mistake for being:

What if instead of enduring entities — you and me, Earth and Sun — there are only the instants, the interactions? Only the burgeoning, interconnected, multilayered meshwork of creative sparks? From those sparks emerge selves and worlds — our private worlds of perception but also shared frameworks and structures: social, cultural, historical, scientific. Each instant, all of it is born and reborn.

Our experience of Now, I’m convinced, is not a hallucination. With every detail we choose to attend to, to breathe life into, we’re helping to write into existence both ourselves and the world… What if the universe wasn’t created in one Big Bang but, as Wheeler put it, “in billions upon billions of tiny creative flashes that are sounding out all around us”? This journey into Now has made me wonder whether reality might have given us not just one long-ago moment of creation but an ongoing miracle.

Perhaps, with our help, the whole universe is continually being made and remade. And the future isn’t written after all.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

ADA, XRP, DOGE: Gemini Names Biggest Losers of 2026

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Crypto exchange Gemini has unveiled the biggest losers in the altcoin space for 2026 so far, as many of the crypto world's biggest digital currencies have been seeing massive double-digit price plunges.

Crypto exchange Gemini recently shared a list of 10 cryptocurrencies that have seen particularly brutal losses since the start of 2026 in a post on the X social media platform.

Topping Gemini's list of the biggest losers for this year is Bitcoin Cash (BCH), which has crashed a shocking 61.76% year-to-date.

Coming in second is Polkadot (DOT), with a 53.03% price decline, while Cardano (ADA) holds third place after shedding 48.04% of its value.

Ripple-related XRP and the top meme cryptocurrency Dogecoin (DOGE) are next in line, with losses of 43.43% and 42.38%, respectively.

Several other major cryptocurrencies are also seeing considerable losses, such as Sui (SUI), which has fallen 42.08%, Hedera (HBAR), which has dropped 38.13%, Solana (SOL), down 34.02%, Avalanche (AVAX), which has lost 33.40%, and Kaspa (KAS), which has gone down 30.75%.

Declining liquidity in the market, weakening speculative interest, lackluster institutional interest and general macroeconomic conditions are all major factors causing most major altcoins to underperform so far.

But for some, other issues within their ecosystems are also proving troublesome.

For instance, in the case of Cardano, the platform is seeing limited decentralized finance activity. As of Nov. 9, the total value locked in its DeFi ecosystem reportedly dropped below $50 million.

While Ripple continues its expansion in the institutional payments sector, stablecoin and tokenization areas, this hasn't necessarily led to sustained buying interest in XRP.

In fact, spot trading volume for XRP fell 53.5%, while perpetual futures trading volume dropped 44%.

Dogecoin, on the other hand, has been contending with waning speculative interest in meme cryptocurrencies.

Its institutional investment narrative also hasn't really caught on yet, with Bitwise announcing in September the shutdown of its Dogecoin ETF as the product failed to draw investor interest.

Bitcoin Cash has been seeing particularly sharp declines as it is now experiencing a reversal of its stellar 2025 performance, when the cryptocurrency soared around 38%.

Selling pressure picked up in May and June 2026 as BCH saw back-to-back monthly drops of over 30%.

A rebound could be seen in September after CME Group announced Bitcoin Cash futures, but this proved insufficient to stop the ongoing downtrend.

All of Adobe Camera RAW’s Awesome Updates Adobe Didn’t Mention

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

All of Adobe Camera RAW’s Awesome Updates Adobe Didn’t Mention

The good news keeps coming for photographers using Adobe’s software. Alongside big Lightroom Classic updates, Adobe Camera Raw (ACR) has been updated today to version 19.0, ushering in a wide range of tweaks, some of which are major.

Adobe Camera Raw is an extremely important part of Adobe Photoshop for photography workflows, especially for photographers who capture RAW photos. The underlying RAW processing and editing technologies found in ACR overlap heavily with Lightroom, although sometimes a feature arrives first in ACR, like Glow, which debuted in Adobe Camera Raw a few months ago before coming to Lightroom today. Another example is Reflection Removal, which arrived first in Adobe Camera Raw before eventually making it into other Adobe photography apps.

Speaking of Glow, it has been upgraded today. There are new Motion and Streak styles. Users can fine-tune the length, direction, color, and softness of the new Glow variants.

In terms of all-new Adobe Camera Raw features, it’s a bulky list. Virtual Copies, a longtime feature for creating multiple versions of photos inside Lightroom without duplicating original files, are now in Adobe Camera Raw. Each version’s edits can be preserved between sessions as well.

Generative Remove selections can now be applied to all selected photos inside a batch within ACR. So, for example, if a photographer has a group of photos that all have the same distracting element, such as a telephone pole, it’s possible to select the element in one photo, remove it, and then apply the same process to the entire group without needing to manually mask and remove it from each shot.

It’s also now possible to organize photos inside an ACR session. Related or similar photos can be grouped into stacks manually or automatically using capture metadata. It’s worth noting that Auto-Stack by Capture Metadata is currently only supported for recent Canon and Nikon cameras that support the DNG file format. However, all photographers can still group photos into stacks manually.

For those who use Adobe Camera Raw to process many photos at once, navigating through a crowded filmstrip or Grid view can be tedious. ACR 19.0 lets users temporarily hide photos or show only selected shots during a session, which significantly cleans up the workspace.

Finally, Adobe Camera Raw 19.0 introduces new camera and lens support. The list of newly supported cameras is very short, including just the two cameras on the new Apple iPhone Duo. However, new lens support has arrived for Sony’s new FE 8-14mm f/3.5 Fisheye G lens, FE 400mm f/4.5 GM and FE 600mm f/6.3 GM telephoto primes, and new zooms from Sigma and Tamron. There’s also support for Laowa’s 50mm f/2.8 2x Ultra-Macro APO lens across many mounts.

There’s no card that earns more for ongoing spending than the Bilt Palladium Card (See rates and fees). It’s a $495 annual fee card, and that I think that easily makes sense if you’re spending $25,000 or more a year on the card (that’s just a little over $2,000 a month).

But not everyone is going to invest in an expensive card. I talk most about products like this, and the $795 annual fee Chase Sapphire Reserve® (See rates and fees).

I think it’s really worth looking at mid-tier cards, also. Something more expensive than the no annual fee products, where you can still do quite well, but if you’re willing to invest a little but you can get outsized return.

I love Capital One Venture X Rewards Credit Card (See rates and fees) for lounge access, and I think it’s easily worth the $395 annual fee considering it’s effectively rebated in value by the $300 annual credit for bookings through Capital One Travel and 10,000 bonus miles (equal to $100 towards travel) every year, starting on your first anniversary.

But if you don’t want to spend that much on an annual fee then Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card (See rates and fees) still earns at least 2X miles on all purchases and they’re transferrable points. It has a $95 annual fee.

Ink Business Preferred® Credit Card (See rates and fees) has an offer to earn 100k bonus points after you spend $8,000 on purchases in the first 3 months from account opening.

What’s great is earning 3x (on the first $150,000 spent in combined purchases) on travel, shipping purchases, internet, cable and phone services, advertising purchases made with social media sites and search engines each account anniversary year.

It has the same points transfer partners as Chase Sapphire Reserve®, for a lot less money, and a lot of the points-earning for spend. And if you have some of the no annaul fee Chase cards, you can move the points from those cards over to Ink Business Preferred and then transfer them to airlines and hotel programs.

Citi Strata Premier® Card (See rates and fees.) This isn’t the ultra-premium Strate Elite. But you still earn 60,000 bonus ThankYou® Points after spending $4,000 in the first 3 months of account opening.

Where it shines is as a tool to earn for your spending at the $95 annual fee price point: Earn 10 Points per $1 spent on Hotels, Car Rentals, and Attractions booked on CitiTravel.com; Earn 3 Points per $1 on Air Travel and Other Hotel Purchases, at Restaurants, Supermarkets, Gas and EV Charging Stations; Earn 1 Point per $1 spent on all other purchases.

And points transfer to American Airlines AAdvantage, here’s the full list of partners:

Wells Fargo Autograph Journey is a sleeper because it’s a $95 annual fee card that earns 5x on hotels, 4x on airlines, and 3x on restaurants and other travel. That means it still bonuses Airbnb and cruises, which Sapphire Reserve does not. And they give you a $50 annual statement credit with a $50+ airline purchase each year, cutting the effective cost of that annual fee.

Where the card isn’t as strong is points transfers. They’ve promised more, but that’s happened very slowly. They transfer to the IAG Avios programs (Aer Lingus, British Airways, Iberia) as well as Air France KLM, Cathay Pacific, JetBlue, Virgin, Wyndham and Choice.

Bilt Obsidian Card (See rates and fees) is a $95 annual fee card that will earn up to 1.25X points on rent and mortgage payments with no transaction fee.

It’s great for card spending because it earns 3X on your choice of grocery (up to $25K/year) or dining. Your 3X category choice remains in effect for the entire calendar year. It also earns 2X points on travel. And it comes with a $100 Bilt Travel Hotel credit. Applied twice a year, as $50 statement credits, for qualifying Bilt Travel Portal hotel bookings.

The reason this is so strong is that, combined with points for rent or mortgage, you can be earning more than 4 points per dollar in your chosen category and these are the most valuable points and Bilt runs the biggest transfer bonuses. They partner with programs like Hyatt, United, Southwest, Alaska, and Japan Airlines. You don’t have to spend $395, $795 or $895 to earn valuable rewards. Which one of these works best for you depends on what your spend pattern looks like. Ink Business Preferred® Credit Card is probably best if you’re in a position to get a small business card. Wells Fargo Autograph Journey is great for heavy travel spend, if their transfer partners work for you. Citi Strata Premier® Card probably offers the best return for the way most people spend. While the Bilt Obsidian Card has the most potential upside if you play your hand well. Stephen Miller Into the Breach: How Susie Wiles’s Failures Portend Miller’s Future

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

There’s a time-honored tradition for new White House chiefs of staff: The outgoing chief invites former holders of the office, Republicans and Democrats, to a get-together (usually in the West Wing), where the newcomer is offered advice. But Donald Trump’s new chief, Stephen Miller—appointed in a jaw-dropping Truth Social post on Friday—won’t be getting a party. First, because the tradition applies only to a president’s first chief. Second, because no Democratic chief would likely want to be caught dead with Miller, a longtime basher of the opposition party. Shortly after hearing that Trump had picked the hard-right firebrand as his new chief, James Carville sent me a text that summed up the prevailing attitude: “This is like Caligula’s appointment of his horse as consul.”

It was different when Susie Wiles came to town. On the evening of November 19, 2024, she was the guest of honor at a dinner party thrown by Jeffrey Zients, Joe Biden’s outgoing White House chief of staff. They were joined by eight of Zients’s predecessors. It was an occasion for bipartisan bonhomie, a tradition started in 2008 by George W. Bush’s chief Joshua Bolten. In a spirit of goodwill and generosity, Bolten had thrown a gathering in his West Wing office for Barack Obama’s incoming chief, Rahm Emanuel. The informal meetup was intended as a way to convey the chiefs’ collective wisdom and experience—and to offer, in miniature, a nuts-and-bolts version of the transition of executive power that is a hallmark of American democracy.

Now evicted from her West Wing corner office, with the president’s agenda in tatters, Wiles has learned that fealty to Trump almost always ends badly.

On the night of the dinner for Wiles, however, a cloud hung over the occasion. Virtually no one at the table thought she could succeed as Donald Trump’s chief of staff.

For almost two years, Wiles was the second most powerful person in the West Wing. Had Trump been interested in governing, she might have helped him solidify a GOP majority in Congress for years to come. But she was unwilling, or unable, to save Trump from himself. On Wiles’s watch, he squandered his second term with a reign of revenge, retribution, and recklessness. Now evicted from her West Wing corner office, with the president’s agenda in tatters, Wiles has learned that fealty to Trump almost always ends badly.

And if past experience is any guide, Wiles’s replacement, Stephen Miller, may well crash and burn in even more spectacular fashion.

There was no one the president relied on or trusted more than Wiles. From their first meeting in 2015 at Trump Tower to her impressive stint as his winning 2024 presidential campaign co-manager, the “ice maiden” had Trump’s back. And he had hers—until last Tuesday’s epic Democratic blowout. Wiles would resign two days later. Indeed, the Republicans’ midterm apocalypse was an extinction event that not even Wiles could have survived.

Ironically, Trump’s savvy political strategist was the architect of what turned out to be a big blue bloodbath. “We’re going to win the midterms,” Wiles told me boldly when I interviewed her for Vanity Fair just a year ago. Even then, three months before Trump initiated a war on Iran, that was a ridiculously rosy prediction. The president’s approval rating was in the gutter. He’d failed to address, much less solve, voters’ primary concerns: affordability, inflation, health care, and housing—and had still not released the remaining Epstein files. How could a battle-hardened political pro like Wiles be so starry-eyed? The answer, I believe, is that in the fog of MAGA-land, she’d lost touch with the real world.

In almost every presidency, the West Wing is a bubble where like-minded acolytes engage in groupthink. But in Trump’s West Wing this phenomenon is exponentially magnified. It’s a parallel universe, one in which the 2020 election was stolen, violent insurrectionists are “patriots,” and the prosecution of political enemies—with scant evidence—is a presidential perk. (“Who would blame him?” Wiles said to me of Trump’s campaign to settle scores.) That, to my mind, is why Wiles seemed so stunned by the firestorm that erupted when her words appeared in my 10,000-word Vanity Fair series last December. She had no idea they would land like bombs on planet Earth.

Wiles invested Trump with almost otherworldly powers. He would lead the GOP to victory in the 2026 midterms, she believed, because of his preternatural ability to rally his base. That’s why, on the eve of the midterms, Wiles was all-in on sending Trump almost everywhere all at once, admonishing people to “vote like I’m on the ballot.” The trouble was voters saw all too clearly what she apparently couldn’t see: Trump was deeply unpopular; on his watch, the country was coming apart.

A year ago, when Wiles made her electoral prediction, I challenged her. “I admire your confidence about the midterms,” I said, “but the stakes are huge, right? And history is not encouraging. Incumbents do stuff and there’s a reaction. And usually, as Obama said, you get shellacked.”

Wiles seemed to shrug off that possibility. “Can you ever imagine Donald Trump saying that?” she said. “No, no.”

Wiles was right. Trump could never say such a thing—because he could never accept the fact that his electoral magic had waned. Neither, it seemed, could Wiles. But as his chief of staff and top political strategist, she was paid to know better.

Even before the election debacle, Trump had reportedly started privately inquiring about her on phone calls, although he lashed out at accounts of such purported conversations. Then, once the carnage sunk in, the die was cast. Wiles offered her resignation. And Trump accepted it.

At the start of her West Wing tenure, I believed Wiles stood a chance of succeeding. If Trump would listen to anyone, I thought, it was the loyal, organized, no-nonsense Wiles. Yet during 10 conversations with her over 10 months, I watched as she abdicated the most important duty of the White House chief: telling the president hard truths. Wiles told me she’d read my book, The Gatekeepers: How the White House Chiefs of Staff Define Every Presidency, and she understood the importance of heeding Reagan chief James Baker’s advice. “You do not serve your president well if you are just a yes man,” he said. In a phone call, she told me, Baker “reminded me that my job wasn’t to be a fangirl.” I asked Wiles, “Have you had any gut check moments where you had to tell the president something he didn’t want to hear?” She paused. “I probably have one a day,” she said. “But they’re over little things, not big.”

Wiles should have known that the midterms would spell disaster. She carried around a list of her priorities, on an index card. Number five: “Don’t forget about organized labor. Don’t forget about young Black men and women and Hispanics…because that’s the future of the country and the backbone of his support. And if we don’t convert those people to being enthusiastic Republicans, then if the Democrats were even just a little bit better, they might win an election.”

Miller is a zealot, a hard-boiled ideologue—an advocate of Trump’s notorious first-term Muslim ban, the brutal paramilitary tactics of ICE, the deployment of the National Guard to Democrat-run cities, and the lethal air strikes on boats in the Caribbean.

They might—and they did. And now Wiles is out, taking up shop, so Trump himself has said, within his political operation. Last November she hinted that she knew this day might come: “You go to bed at night, you say your prayers, you take stock of what the day was and what your day is tomorrow, and you get up and do it again…. So as long as that’s the case and the president’s pleased…” Wiles paused. “He could fire you tomorrow.”

She’s sensitive about her legacy as the first female White House chief. “I’m not an enabler,” she insisted. “I’m also, like, not a bitch. I try to be thoughtful about what I engage in, and what I disagree [with the president] about. I guess time will tell whether I’ve been effective.”

What about Wiles’s successor, Stephen Miller?

Miller is the antithesis of Ronald Reagan’s chief James Baker, a cool pragmatist who could push back on the president’s half-baked ideas. Miller, by contrast, is a zealot, a hard-boiled ideologue—an advocate of Trump’s notorious first-term Muslim ban, the brutal paramilitary tactics of ICE, the deployment of the National Guard to Democrat-run cities, and the lethal air strikes on boats in the Caribbean. He believes in a Hobbesian world, he has stated, “that is governed by strength, that is governed by force, that is governed by power.” Now he’ll be the last person in the room when Trump makes life-and-death decisions about law and order and war and peace.

The man who decreed that the president’s powers “are very substantial and will not be questioned” is now the official charged with questioning the president’s decisions. It’s hard to imagine Miller doing that without a personality transplant. He’s already alienated the House minority leader, and soon to be Speaker, Hakeem Jeffries, who has called Miller “a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands,” insisting, “I’ve got zero interest in working with that kind of individual.”

At the White House last November, after a Vanity Fair photo session, I spoke with Miller at length about the midterms, still a year away; about his portfolio in the Trump administration; and about the role of the White House chief of staff. Perched on a chair in the Roosevelt Room, he eyed me warily. He seemed impatient to get back to work. “I understand that your need for me is quite limited, right?” he said.

I asked Miller about his interactions with the president—specifically an Oval Office meeting in which he and Trump reportedly talked about blowing up boats in the Caribbean. “I would never talk about…whether a meeting occurred or didn’t occur,” he said, “or whether it was in a meeting that did occur, or was not in that meeting if it occurred, which I would never say either way.”

Miller was more forthcoming about Wiles’s prediction—that Trump and the GOP would triumph at the polls in 2026. “What about you?” I asked him. “I am confident that we’ll win the midterms,” he replied. History might be against them, he said, “but we have every expectation that we’re going to defy that history.” “Why?” I asked. Miller replied, “Because of the president’s unprecedentedly successful first nine months in office.”

What followed was a black-is-white, up-is-down litany of Trump’s accomplishments, a revisionist history that might have made Karoline Leavitt, another world-class propagandist, blush. “In no particular order,” Miller said, “the taming and defeating of inflation. As you know, we inherited record double-digit inflation and the president made his first priority to bring down the cost of living…cutting the price of energy by high double digits. For many Americans, gas prices have been cut in half since we took over.” None of this, of course, has proved true—but Miller was just warming up.

“And the passage of the largest package of tax cuts and reforms in American history in the form of the One Big Beautiful Bill. Fully securing the Southern border, the most secure border in history, zero illegal crossings, four months in a row. And also the largest number of deportations in American history…. The president has…achieved more in foreign policy than any president in our lifetimes, ending eight wars in eight months—which includes, but is not limited to, securing the historic peace deal between Israel and Gaza, Thailand and Cambodia, Congo and Rwanda, Azerbaijan and Armenia, Israel and Iran, and so on…”

Miller still wasn’t done. The president, he said, “has executed on every promise he’s made to the American people and that includes also taking back our culture and fighting against whether you call it radical leftism or woke or Marxism—a lot of people just call it plain crazy—but taking back our culture and restoring sanity to American life.

“It is my extraordinary privilege and blessing,” Miller added, “to be able to work for the president of the United States, to implement an agenda that I believe in wholly and completely and with every fiber of my being.”

I was there, of course, because I was writing a story on Susie Wiles and her West Wing team. I asked him, point-blank: “Would you agree, though, that it’s also the chief of staff’s job to tell the president what he doesn’t want to hear?”

“No, I wouldn’t say that,” Miller replied, finding fault with my framing of the question and insisting that this president wants to hear everything. “The chief of staff would never have to encounter the scenario you’re describing.” Miller said. “President Trump craves, he loves the every-minute detail of management, having every piece of information, having every side of everything, having inputs into the Oval Office from every square, every quarter, every person.”

As for the president’s staff, Miller said, their job is “implementing the president’s vision, the president’s directions, the president’s agenda, the president’s plans for the future…. We’re just here on his behalf to see that that is done.”

As the great political columnist Molly Ivins once wrote, “It probably sounded better in the original German.”

Chris Whipple is a contributing writer at Vanity Fair and a Fall 2026 Resident Fellow at Harvard’s Kennedy School Institute of Politics. You can follow him on Substack.

Influencer uses his 1.1M followers to track down stolen $500K McLaren

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A Detroit influencer managed to recover his stolen $500,000 McLaren after using his 1.1 million social media followers to track it down, but not before thieves were caught doing donuts in the luxury supercar.

Businessman and content creator Ali Sobh was left stunned when his rare McLaren disappeared from the driveway behind his home in Detroit’s Boston-Edison neighborhood.

However, things took an even stranger turn when footage surfaced online showing someone driving his stolen vehicle at a street takeover, performing donuts in front of a crowd of spectators.

Rather than simply waiting for police to locate the car, Sobh decided to put his massive social media following to work.

According to ClickOnDetroit, Sobh shared footage of the stolen vehicle with his 1.1 million followers and asked for help identifying its whereabouts.

The footage, also highlighted by FOX 2 Detroit, showed the McLaren being driven recklessly at a street takeover, with the driver spinning the expensive vehicle around in front of onlookers.

A post shared by Ali Sobh (@alisobh_official)

Fortunately, Sobh’s followers came through with tips that helped narrow down the car’s location.

The influencer passed a potential location near Six Mile and Telegraph to Detroit police, who subsequently recovered the $500,000 supercar.

Sobh explained that the vehicle was particularly special because of its rare paint job.

“I’m the only one in the country that has this color as well,” he told ClickOnDetroit.

Although he managed to recover the vehicle, Sobh was understandably frustrated that the thieves had been brazen enough to show it off at a public gathering.

The incident is another bizarre addition to the growing list of social media stars having problems with expensive vehicles.

Previously, YouTuber WhistlinDiesel helped recover a fan’s stolen truck, while influencer Duke Dennis had his Lamborghini broken into and personal belongings stolen.

Detroit police are continuing to investigate the theft and review footage from the street takeover to identify those responsible.

No arrests have been announced, and Sobh says he plans to increase security around his home to prevent another incident.

PSI is trying to make a business out of AI-driven scientific discovery

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

David Turturean, a recent MIT graduate and a member of the technical staff at the startup lab Physical Superintelligence (PSI), used early access to Anthropic’s Fable 5.2 model to solve an 18-year-old problem about quantum computing.

“I’m totally accepting the idea that this might be the last paper I ever put out,” Turturean, who deferred plans to pursue a master’s at MIT’s prestigious computer science and AI lab to join PSI, told TechCrunch. “In a few months, like, why would you even put out a paper?”

The problem in question, the Aaronson-Ambainis conjecture, concerns the ability of quantum computers to solve problems faster than classical ones. Scott Aaronson, one of the two researchers who posed the problem, writes that he doesn’t quite understand the proof but that he believes it’s correct because it was autoformalized in Lean, a coding language for math, and explained by a team of top researchers with whom PSI shared the solution.

The solution appears to close the door on one avenue quantum computers could follow to solve certain difficult problems at high speed, but not all of them. “It’s a negative result that allows people to know, okay, we’re no longer looking there,” Turturean said.

More broadly, it reflects the upheaval in the scientific world caused by AI problem-solving.

“A proof of full [Aaronson-Ambainis conjecture] was one of the last remaining open problems in quantum query complexity that everyone seemed to agree was centrally important,” Aaronson, who described himself feeling satisfied, awestruck, disappointed, and fatigued by the news, wrote. “I was surprised, honestly, to see it missing from OpenAI’s Mathocalypse dump a few weeks ago. Surely they tried it, so was it really so much harder than all the problems that were solved?”

That’s a point in favor of PSI’s model, which is to solve scientific problems with specialized model harnesses and scaffolding.

“An effective kind of research system should have lots of things related to harnesses, lots related to tools, memory systems, simulation infrastructure,” PSI CEO Matt Pines told TechCrunch. “You want to be able to really go after a problem at breadth and depth, and be able to consider a wide range of potential alternatives.”

For now, the company’s revenue comes from using its tools to optimize data centers, simulating the variables of heat, power, water, sound — “where the performance of that site as well as its impact on the community are a direct function of its physics.” The long play, however, is converting theoretical physics breakthroughs into actual technologies.

“Six to nine months ago, when I was talking to investors, like I had to sort of stretch to validate that conditional, right? Like, ‘no, no, no, trust me, pretty soon, theoretical physics is going to be mostly the domain of machines,’” Pines said. “And it’s not there yet, but it’s on its way.”

Still, while AI has claimed important solutions in fields like mathematics and computer science where it’s possible to express them in validated code, doing so in less abstracted disciplines like the life sciences or physics has proven more difficult. Last week, the startup Discovered Materials released a $1 million grand challenge for researchers using AI to understand problems like superconductivity and heat dissipation.

“At the end of the day, physics is different than math because it’s the real world … nature gets a vote in your models of reality,” Pines said. He said the startup is focused on understanding how to take computational results and validate them with experiments in the most efficient way possible.

“If it’s a true fact of nature, that’s a trick that you can exploit,” Pines said. “You could build a device out of it, right? The AI suggests an idea; it allows you to do something in the world you weren’t able to do before … and so each of those are kind of loops that PSI is building.”

As “neolabs for science” become a pathway for potentially groundbreaking science, the participants are still figuring out what the standards are for this new world. Because PSI had to hold its result until Anthropic’s model was nearly ready for release, they were able to share it with relevant researchers to better understand it.

“As an academic, we wanted to play our role in doing things the right way,” Jim Halverson, a professor at Northeastern University who advises PSI, said. He’ll be attending a summit at MIT next week where leading physicists will try to hash out a response to the new pace of science. “Best practices are still emerging … We figured that transparency is a good way to go, and contacting experts is a good way to go.”

“We had some time to sit on it … and it was the first time that I actually took one of my AI-elicited solutions on print, and I read through it, and I was like, okay, how can I make this … be presented more naturally for a human?” Turturean, who said the academic community will expect him to be able to explain the result, told TechCrunch. “And I think it also made me realize, oh my God, the weight of what AI can do.”

Why This 251-Year-Old Watchmaker Is Suddenly Hotter Than Ever in 2026

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Welcome to GQ’s 201-level Watch Glossary, breaking down the brands, advanced terms, and specific references that are essential knowledge for every serious collector.

Founded in the late 18th century by perhaps the world’s most famous watchmaker, Abraham Louis-Breguet, the eponymous brand was once the horologer to royalty. (He famously made a watch for Marie Antoinette that she unfortunately, ahem, never got the chance to wear.) Breguet is responsible for the tourbillon, an early self-winding mechanism, his famous namesake numerals that nearly every brand in Switzerland uses now, and much more. But despite its massive contributions to horology, the brand's pieces haven't caused frenzies among collectors like those from Rolex, Patek Philippe, or even Cartier. However, like Dune’s Bene Gesserit, the watchmaker’s plans are apparently measured in centuries. Recent signals point to the idea that Breguet may be entering a new golden era as collectors come around to its exquisitely made timepieces.

Follow the money, and you’ll quickly find evidence of Breguet’s glow-up. Late last month, at Monaco Legends Group’s fall watch auction, a 1970s-era piece previously owned by a 50-year veteran Breguet watchmaker sold for €221,000. That was nearly of the beginning of the brand's impressive run of auction that makes us wonder, What’s going on with Breguet these days?

Abraham Louis-Breguet isn’t the only hallowed watchmaker to steer the brand. Legendary names like George Daniels, F.P. Journe, and Michel Parmigiani have contributed to the brand’s legacy. At auction, many collectors are keying in on a period of Breguet led by Daniel Roth from the mid-’70s to 1987. After he left Breguet, Roth went on to found his own successful brand that now sells for six figures at auction. The combination of these two powers is proving irresistible to today’s collectors.

The Ref. 3050A was a signature piece of Roth’s reign at Breguet and epitomizes the designer’s contributions to the brand. Roth introduced the first modern piece from the manufacturer with a perpetual calendar. The piece’s guilloché dial is also a signature of the Roth era. The watch sold for €85,800 at the end of October.

The 3057 was the successor to Roth’s first watch at Breguet, the 3050. The big change is the transparent caseback. This lovely semi-skeletonized perpetual calendar hammered for some 200% above its estimate—not a huge surprise considering Breguet made only a couple of these a year when they were in production. Notably, thanks to the addition of that exhibition caseback, we can see the pretty, highly decorated automatic movement.

It’s platinum. It’s skeletonized. It’s got a tourbillon. It’s got a matching bracelet. It’s accompanied by all its factory goodies. What else could one ask for in a pre-owned Breguet? Still, a $387,000 hammer price is a remarkable outcome.

Classique Grandes Complications ref. 5447

This oversized perpetual-calendar minute repeater, likewise executed in platinum, commanded almost $271,000 at Phillips this weekend—proof positive of the appeal of classical Breguet looks mixed with modern movement technology and finishing.

An evolution of the Daniel Roth-era ref. 3350, this gorgeous tourbillon wristwatch features one of the hallmarks of Breguet’s signature style: a beautifully guilloché dial combined with blued steel hands and a Roman-numeral minute track.

Breguet took a significant left turn in the 1950s when it began producing the Type XX flyback chronograph for French military aviators. It still serves as an important pillar in the brand’s lineup today.

Introduced in the late 1980s, the ref. 3350 is Breguet’s first wrist-worn tourbillon—a significant milestone for the brand that invented the complication. Emblematic of the Daniel Roth era of Breguet production, it has since become a neo-vintage classic.

Released in 2025 in celebration of the firm’s 250th birthday, the Classique Souscription uses a single hand to tell the time against a Grand Feu enamel dial mirroring vintage Breguet pocket watches. Just this week, the maison followed it up with the platinum version seen above.

The Expérimentale 1 is the first step in a new collection of envelope-pushing, ultra-complicated watches. Fitted with advanced materials, the “1” features watchmaking’s very first high-frequency tourbillon, which beats at an astounding 10 Hz. This is a great way forward for modern Breguet—a model that embraces the brand’s tradition as the industry's most inventive watchmaker.

Now a quarter-millennium old, Breguet has one of the most extensive histories of any watchmaker still in operation, with records reaching back generations. Its finishing is exceptional; its complications are rooted in some of the earliest experiments by one of the world’s most famous horologists; and its designs are superb. As prices continue to skyrocket for watches at auction, especially for hotshot newcomers, it’s exciting to see one of the most storied brands in history catch fire, too.

DeepSeek AI XRP Price Prediction: Could Ripple Really Go This High?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Ripple (XRP USD) is trading near recent lows, yet the setup for an extraordinary re-rating remains intact if one landmark catalyst materializes. According to a DeepSeek AI XRP price prediction, while price action has been heavy and sentiment has been cautious, a highly unlikely but transformative development could still rewrite the valuation story.

Imagine a coordinated announcement in which a major international payments consortium, backed by several large commercial banks, a key global messaging network, and supportive regulatory frameworks in multiple jurisdictions, formally designates the XRP Ledger as a preferred settlement and liquidity layer for high-value cross-border flows and tokenized deposit networks.

In this scenario, participating institutions would be incentivized (or in some cases required under new capital and liquidity rules) to hold meaningful XRP balances as bridge liquidity and collateral. The resulting structural demand would far exceed current free-float dynamics and push the asset into true price discovery.

XRP is currently consolidating in the $1.08–$1.15 region after a multi-week decline that has tested lower supports. The chart shows a series of lower highs, yet longer-term support zones from prior consolidation bases remain intact for now.

Volume has been mixed, with selling pressure not yet reaching capitulation extremes. A sustained recovery above the $1.30–$1.40 area would mark an important shift in short-term structure and open the path toward previous intermediate highs.

In an extreme adoption-driven scenario, measured-move extensions from the current base and multi-year Fibonacci projections could theoretically support significantly higher targets once momentum and institutional flows align.

Momentum indicators remain depressed, leaving substantial room for expansion if buying returns. Even as the broader market digests recent weakness, the combination of real institutional product progress and the possibility of a structural demand shock keeps the longer-term bullish case alive for patient holders.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Euro stablecoins have grown from ~€50M to €450M since early 2024. Against a ~$300B stablecoin market, that’s still a fraction of what’s possible.Qivalis, a consortium of 37 European banks now authorized by De Nederlandsche Bank, is set to change that. But issuance is only…

Despite the soft price action, Ripple continues to expand its institutional footprint. RLUSD has seen steady growth in market capitalization and adoption, with listings and settlement use cases emerging on platforms including Coinbase and planned integrations with firms such as Robinhood.

The OCC’s final approval for Ripple National Trust Bank strengthens the regulatory foundation for its stablecoin and related services. Additional developments include custody support discussions with major institutions, tokenization initiatives (such as fund share classes on the XRP Ledger), and ongoing technical work on the ledger itself, including proposals aimed at long-term security enhancements.

These building blocks do not guarantee an immediate price response, but they reinforce the infrastructure narrative that has historically underpinned XRP’s strongest cycles. In a broader market recovery, this accumulated progress could matter.

When the route gets complicated, Hyper gets creative. ⚡️🔥https://t.co/VNG0P4FWNQ pic.twitter.com/NMYjdRGchd

While DeepSeek AI predicts an uber-bullish move for XRP crypto, Bitcoin Hyper (HYPER) is more reliably focused on another significant opportunity: bringing high-speed financial applications to the Bitcoin ecosystem.

Bitcoin Hyper aims to capitalize on Bitcoin’s significant liquidity through a Layer 2 network designed for faster transactions and decentralized finance (DeFi) functionality. Its architecture uses the Solana Virtual Machine (SVM), bringing Solana-style execution capabilities to the Bitcoin-focused ecosystem.

Bitcoin Hyper’s goal is straightforward: to combine Bitcoin’s substantial liquidity and established investor base with transaction speeds that meet the demands of modern decentralized applications.

Key features include near-instant transaction finality and low costs, which are essential to its market positioning. HYPER is gaining attention through its presale, which has reportedly raised over $33.2M, indicating strong early fundraising momentum.

It offers a 35% annual percentage yield (APY) for staking rewards, providing an additional incentive for early participants. Token holders can stake their HYPER tokens to earn extra rewards rather than leaving their allocation idle.

With the planned launch date of November 26 approaching, the presale is entering a crucial phase. Those interested in Bitcoin’s expanding Layer 2 ecosystem are eagerly anticipating Hyper’s market debut before it gets listed on centralized exchanges.

By January 1, 2027, in this over-the-top bullish scenario, XRP could reach $28 – $45, with a stretch target above $60 if the global payments and liquidity adoption narrative fully takes hold.

This remains pure speculation and entertainment, not a realistic base-case outlook. The catalyst would require extraordinary and coordinated regulatory action across major economies and financial institutions that is currently highly unlikely.

Inflation Hotter Than Economist Expectations, Raising Odds of 2027 Rate Hikes

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Inflation picked up speed in October, as the latest Consumer Price Index (CPI) from the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) accelerated faster than predictions from economists.

The October CPI saw the all-items index grow 0.4%, a slight decrease from last month’s 0.6% rise, but “hotter than expected,” according to Navy Federal Chief Economist Heather Long. Original forecasts were at 0.2% to 0.3% increases, as noted by Realtor.com® Senior Economist Jake Krimmel.

Inflation’s jump this month was due to increases in shelter inflation (up 0.3%), contributing to over 30% of the increase. Food inflation also advanced in October by 0.5%. Energy inflation, which decelerated in October, still saw a rise of 0.7%.

This month’s data does not include annual inflation numbers as the BLS noted it did not collect data in October 2025 due to the government being shut down at the time. As a result, it cannot calculate 12-month percent changes.

With the data available, Long said October’s increase “implies about a ~4% inflation rate for the past year.”

The index for all items less food and energy—core inflation—grew by 0.3% in October, the same rate of growth it saw in both September and August. Indexes that contributed to increase include household furnishings and operations, recreation, medical care, motor vehicle insurance and education.

Long said this month’s data “underscores why so many Americans voted the way they did in the mid-term elections,” speaking of the “blue wave” that overtook the elections last week.

“The same supply-side and geopolitical pressures are still at work, with energy playing an important role and tariff-driven goods inflation not going away,” added Krimmel. “These are the costs most salient to consumers and voters, as we saw, yet the Fed has less direct control over them.”

He noted that for the Fed outlook, a “December hike now looks almost certain, with odds jumping to 99% this morning.”

“Additional hikes in early 2027 look more in play as well, with the 10-year Treasury up 9 basis points just 25 minutes after the CPI report release,” Krimmel continued.

Looking at housing, Krimmel said that near-term there could be “upward pressure on mortgage rates again” due to rising Treasury yields and the potential of a rate hike from the Fed.

Expanded Gemini Intelligence, HiLight and more

Google just announced its November Pixel Drop, which adds a slew of new features for Pixel phones and watches. Plus, the Pixel Tag officially goes on sale today (look out for our review here).

There's a ton of stuff in the drop, but some highlights include expanded Gemini Intelligence, a new Create My Widget feature and step-by-step workouts on Pixel Watch 3 and newer.

The company also announced its new Google Fi "Super Network." We've got more details about how it works here.

Let's dive into what's available and which devices support these new features.

For the most part, the big features are only coming to the Pixel 10 and Pixel 11 series phones in this update. Some smaller tools are available on Pixel 6 and later phones. Surprise, most of it is AI-related.

Starting with AI, Gemini Intelligence is now available on Pixel 10 phones, including the new Rambler feature, which automatically removes filler words and can format your spoken words into "clean, coherent" text. It also adds automations for specified apps, including grocery ordering, ridesharing and booking reservations.

Create My Widgets enables you to build custom widgets using AI by describing the type of tool you need. Google is also offering curated recommendations.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Gemini Call Screen is on Pixel 11 phones and screens calls on demand. For Pixel 10 and 11 phones, Gemini can make calls for you, doing things like checking inventory, putting items on hold, or making/changing reservations.

Pixel 11 phones are getting Pixel Solver, which uses generative AI to create custom templates and "conversational, multi-step problem solving for everyday" math.

Pixel 9 and above phones are getting Notification Summaries that will recap long conversations, giving you context at a glance.

The highlight feature, pun intended, is HiLight, announced at the Made By Google event in August. When your phone is face down, HiLight will glow to let you know if a favorite contact has sent you a message.

Otherwise, for 6a and above, there's a new Custom Icons style, which uses Nano Banana to generate. Transit Mode 2.0 triggers automatically based on phone sensors.

Pixel Search Updates lets you find content across your apps, web and settings using natural language search directly from the All Apps screen.

Unit Conversion is a module to help you skip toggling between the browser and the calculator. Pixel 6 and above phones are also getting Live Location Sharing for Pixel VIPs.

Finally, Pixel 10 phones are getting Extreme Charging Mode that quickly charges your phones to full.

Your Pixel Watch isn't getting as many features as the phones, but the big one is "Step-by-Step Workouts." Basically, your watch will give you step-by-step instructions for each exercise in your routine. It will also log reps, weights and track metrics from your Watch, or your phone.

This is available on Pixel Watch 3 and newer models.

Google announced that Create My Widgets, Gemini call Screen and Notification Summaries, outlined above, are "coming soon"; no specific date was provided.

Phone to Watch Unlock is also coming soon to Pixel Watch 3 and new models. This feature will let you unlock your watch with a nearby paired phone. No date was provided here either.
