Plaza Premium Opening Hong Kong Arrivals Lounge… With Live Music?!

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Plaza Premium will be opening an arrivals lounge soon at Hong Kong International Airport (HKG). While these facilities are always useful for a shower and breakfast after an overnight flight, it sounds like there might be a bit more planned for this arrivals lounge? I’m certainly intrigued (thanks to Sel_D for flagging this)…

Plaza Premium is expected to open an arrivals lounge at Hong Kong Airport in late November 2026. The lounge will be located on level three of Terminal 2, in the arrivals portion of the terminal, below the Airport Express platform. That means it’ll be landside.

This actually technically represents the reopening of a lounge, as the global lounge operator had such a facility until 2019. At that point it closed due to an expansion project at the airport. Once open, it will be the only arrivals lounge at the airport. Cathay Pacific used to have an arrivals lounge at its hub, but it closed in 2018, and the airline has no plans to reopen such a facility.

The exact entry requirements of the Plaza Premium arrivals lounge in Hong Kong remain to be seen, but like most Plaza Premium lounges, the expectation is that entry will be possible through select credit cards and Priority Pass, and access can also be purchased directly.

Generally we think of an arrivals lounge in terms of its functional purpose, which is to shower, have breakfast, and get some work done, before heading into the city. Many arrivals lounges are only open in the morning, since that’s when there’s the most value. That’s especially true for people who either plan to head to the office, or who are staying at a hotel, where it’s well before the published check-in time.

While it remains to be seen what exactly Plaza Premium’s facility will be like, it sounds like this will be a rather unconventional arrivals lounge, in a good way. The social media post about the new lounge describes it as “Plaza Premium Lounge – Rhythms.” It will offer a “seamless blend of dining, music, wellness, and refreshment.”

In terms of the range of amenities, we’re being promised live music, wellness experiences, refined dining, and private resting spaces. I’m sorry… live music? Like… someone performing music live? In an arrivals lounge? Fascinatingly, the pictures make it look more like an evening-oriented lounge serving cocktails and wine, for lounging around, rather than your typical arrivals lounge.

A post shared by Plaza Premium Lounge (@plazapremiumlounge)

Suffice it to say that I am very interested in what they come up with here. From a monetization standpoint, this seems kind of smart, since it could drum up more business in the afternoons and evenings, when people might not otherwise consider visiting such a facility. Then again, are people really thinking “I just got off a 15 hour flight, what I could really use is a cocktail before heading to my hotel?” Maybe they are, I suppose!

In the coming weeks, Hong Kong Airport will once again have an arrivals lounge, thanks to the opening of a facility by Plaza Premium. Arrivals lounges are usually pretty basic, and are about showers and breakfast. While we’ll see how much of this is fluff rather than actual substance, Plaza Premium is promising what sounds more like a bar vibe, with live music and cocktails. Bring it on, Plaza Premium, I can’t wait to learn more!

What do you make of this Plaza Premium concept?

Study Tracks 3,000 Real-World Psychedelic Journeys

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Neuroscientists at the University of California, San Francisco (UCSF) are launching CANOPY, a global observational study designed to monitor up to 3,000 individuals taking psychedelics in real-world environments. Led by Dr. Robin Carhart-Harris, the initiative moves beyond artificial laboratory settings to evaluate naturalistic use across homes, ceremonies, and retreats.

Backed by up to $8.5 million in total ARPA-H funding across three translational UCSF projects, CANOPY combines longitudinal remote psychological tracking with in-person neuroimaging, EEG, biomaterial sampling, and wearable biometric monitoring to determine how set, setting, and biology shape therapeutic versus adverse outcomes.

Over the past decade, the resurgence of psychedelic medicine has yielded promising clinical trial data for treatment-resistant depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, and substance use disorders. Yet, the vast majority of human psychedelic use takes place far beyond the sterile walls of university medical centers, unfolding instead in private residences, underground therapeutic circles, ceremonial retreats, and festival environments.

Because clinical trials enforce rigid inclusion criteria, standardized dosing, and artificial hospital-room aesthetics, they leave a critical scientific blind spot: how do variable naturalistic settings, individual biological baselines, and subjective intentions influence long-term mental health outcomes?

To bridge this gap between clinical efficacy and real-world effectiveness, neuroscientists at the University of California, San Francisco (UCSF) have initiated CANOPY, a large-scale global observational study.

Funded by the federal Advanced Research Projects Agency for Health (ARPA-H)—an agency within the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services tasked with driving high-impact biomedical breakthroughs—the study will follow up to 3,000 people across the globe to map what psychedelics do to human brains, bodies, and long-term well-being.

CANOPY is led by Dr. Robin Carhart-Harris, PhD, the Ralph Metzner Distinguished Professor of Neurology and Psychiatry at UCSF and a pioneering figure in human psychedelic neuroimaging.

Unlike conventional clinical trials, the researchers do not supply, administer, or recommend any controlled substances. Instead, the study utilizes an observational, citizen-science design, enrolling individuals who have already made independent plans to consume psychedelic compounds.

The study encompasses a broad pharmacological spectrum, tracking users of classical 5-HT2A agonists (psilocybin, LSD, ayahuasca, mescaline), atypical entheogens (ibogaine), empathogens (MDMA), and dissociative anesthetics (ketamine).

“Psychedelics are widely used outside research labs, yet much of what we know about their effects comes from carefully controlled research settings,” said Carhart-Harris. “CANOPY gives us an opportunity to bring scientific rigor to these experiences at a scale we haven’t had before — to understand not just whether psychedelics can be beneficial, but for whom, under what circumstances, and why.”

The vast majority of participants will engage with the study remotely through decentralized digital platforms, completing validated psychological inventories before their planned session, during the immediate integration period, and longitudinally for up to six months afterward.

For participants able to travel to UCSF’s Neurosciences building in San Francisco, CANOPY incorporates deep biological phenotyping:

By combining granular physiological telemetry with longitudinal psychological endpoints, the team aims to parse out why identical doses can trigger transformative emotional healing in one individual and acute psychological distress or enduring anxiety in another.

CANOPY represents one branch of a tri-part, $8.5 million psychedelic research initiative awarded to UCSF by ARPA-H.

The broader initiative includes two parallel clinical projects:

As state, federal, and international jurisdictions reconsider the legal and therapeutic status of entheogens, real-world epidemiological safety data is urgently needed.

By analyzing the complex interactions among pharmacology, psychological vulnerability, social support, and somatic health across thousands of diverse naturalistic sessions, CANOPY will establish an open evidentiary baseline. The findings will assist clinicians in refining patient stratification criteria, guide harm-reduction protocols for non-clinical users, and direct the development of next-generation psychiatric trials.

What is the primary goal of UCSF’s CANOPY study?

CANOPY is an observational study tracking up to 3,000 adults worldwide who are already planning to take psychedelics in real-world settings (such as homes, retreats, or private ceremonies). The goal is to understand how these substances alter the brain, body, and long-term mental health outside of artificial laboratory environments.

Do UCSF researchers provide participants with psychedelics?

No. Researchers do not provide, prescribe, or encourage the acquisition of psychedelics. CANOPY is an observational, non-interventional trial enrolling people who have already scheduled their own personal sessions. The core study is conducted remotely via digital assessments.

What biological measurements are being collected?

In addition to remote surveys tracking mood and well-being for six months, a subset of participants visiting UCSF will undergo structural and functional MRI, EEG recordings, blood and saliva biomarker analysis, and continuous biometric monitoring using wearable health sensors.

United Airlines Executive Says Iconic Dulles Mobile Lounges Will Retire, But “Won’t Be Forgotten.” What Comes Next?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

United Airlines wants Washington Dulles to move beyond its mobile lounges. But when I asked Andrew Nocella whether he actually likes them, his response suggested that retirement may not mean disappearance. Could one become a bar or café? Could a limited transportation role survive? His answer left room for some speculation.

During a press gaggle at Washington Dulles yesterday, I asked United Airlines Executive Vice President and Chief Commercial Officer Andrew Nocella a simple question: does he like the people movers? (it was in the context of their future place at an evolving IAD).

“I love it,” he responded, before explaining:

“The people movers, of course, is part of my history. I can remember, back in the day, coming out to the aircraft through the people mover, so it’s part of my history. But I do understand the need to evolve and change and innovate. And the people movers need to be innovated out. There are better ways to do it. But it will be an important part of the history of Dulles Airport and I can assure you that People Moves, while they will be retired, they won’t be forgotten.”

These vehicles are technically known as mobile lounges, though people movers is a fitting description too and I’ve called them “moon buggies” for years. Whatever you call them, I found that last assurance intriguing, even if we should be careful not to read too much into it.

Nocella did not announce a preservation project or describe a future use. But promising they will not be forgotten invites the question: how might Dulles preserve something so closely associated with the airport while finally moving beyond the need to use it?

My favorite possibility is turning one into a bar or café.

Imagine a retired mobile lounge parked in a suitable location, with its distinctive shape preserved and the interior converted into somewhere to have a drink while watching aircraft. Keep some of the original seating,  add photographs explaining its history, and suddenly an airport frustration becomes an attraction.

I would happily stop for a cappuccino. I suspect plenty of aviation enthusiasts would make a point of visiting too.

There would obviously be practical questions about access, utilities, and where to put it…maybe the wheels would have to be removed. A vehicle designed to transport passengers is not automatically suitable for operating a restaurant. But as a concept, it strikes me as a fun way to preserve a piece of Dulles without requiring anyone to ride it to immigration.

Nostalgia becomes much easier to appreciate when you can choose whether to participate!

Think how cool it would be to take one of these and put it inside the new Polaris Lounge in Concourse E that will be coming in the years ahead.

Another possibility is that the mobile lounges disappear from most journeys but continue in a narrower role.

I wonder, for example, whether there might be a useful way to run passengers between the D and E gates during the airport’s lengthy transition. Could a mobile lounge provide a convenient connection while the rest of the concourse development catches up? It woudl certainly save a lot of walking.

That is a question, not something Nocella proposed and I do realize that keeping an aging fleet operating for a limited purpose could undermine the savings and simplicity that retirement is supposed to deliver.

Still, “retired” can mean different things. Retired from routine passenger service would not necessarily preclude an occasional special ride or some other carefully limited use.

Of course, “won’t be forgotten” may mean something much less elaborate.

A preserved vehicle, a display in the terminal, or a design reference inside a future lounge could honor the mobile lounges without retaining their transportation function.

I would prefer preserving at least one actual vehicle rather than relying exclusively on photographs. These machines are unusual enough that future travelers should have an opportunity to see what generations of Dulles passengers experienced. I still love them and will always love them.

Nocella’s answer was affectionate, but clear about the need for change. He did not offer a retirement date or reveal what remembering the mobile lounges will look like.

But I do hope that assurance becomes something tangible. A mobile lounge café or bar would get my vote, preferably inside the new Polaris Lounge. A useful transitional shuttle or occasional heritage ride could be interesting too and much easier to maintain than a vintage aircraft.

For years, passengers have boarded these vehicles because they had to. It would be wonderful if Dulles eventually gave us a reason to step inside one because we wanted to.

What would you do with a retired Dulles mobile lounge?

James Gunn’s DC Universe Was Weird, Ambitious, and Ultimately Unfinished

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The writer-director is a casualty of Skydance’s acquisition of Warner Bros. He was building to a future that fans will now likely never see, for better or worse.

The hierarchy of power at DC Studios is changing again. On November 9, news broke that writer-director James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran left—later reported as ousted from—their jobs as co-CEOs and co-chairmen of DC Studios. Jeremy Latcham, a former Marvel Studios producer whose past credits include The Avengers and Gunn’s own Guardians of the Galaxy, will replace them on December 1.

Suddenly, DC fans woke up on a Monday to a new regime. But how will we look back on the four years that a prolific genre writer and director, who came up under Lloyd Kaufman’s B-movie studio Troma, oversaw an entire superhero universe?

It was a time of a lot of promise, not all of it fulfilled.

Before we start the eulogies for the DC Universe, it’s important to note that things aren’t over. Yet. Latcham will take over Gunn and Safran’s posts, so DC Studios is still up and running with more movies in the pipeline, many of which bear Gunn’s fingerprints. Man of Tomorrow, for example. The 2027 movie is a direct sequel to Superman, also written and directed by Gunn. Among its biggest moves is introducing a new Wonder Woman (rumored, but not confirmed, to be Adria Arjona). The Batman: Part II, the long-awaited sequel to Matt Reeves’s The Batman, will also open in 2028 with Gunn and Safran involved as producers. Gunn also said on Instagram they have been “engaged on another DC project which I’m writing.”

Both Gunn and Safran are expected to stay at DC, with Deadline confirming they will “continue to produce on various DC projects.” That said, Matthew Belloni at Puck published a story citing sources who say the DCU will reboot, again, after Man of Tomorrow. And at The Hollywood Reporter, Borys Kit and Aaron Crouch detailed the friction Gunn faced while at the studio, and how Gunn and Safran’s exits were forced by new Skydance leaders David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz. THR reports the DC heads felt secure after the big Warner-Paramount merger; Clayface had grossed $100 million, and Gunn was preparing a draft for the third Superman movie when they were told they were out. You can say the DCU is in a crisis right now.

For now, we can say that a critical chapter of DC is over. Gunn will no longer apply his vision and filter across all DC projects, which he must feel a little relieved about. By his own admission, which he wrote on Instagram on November 9, Gunn expressed feeling stretched thin, having to exert energy on movies and shows he wasn’t emotionally invested in to begin with. Said Gunn: “[T]he thing my ADHD hyperfocus has had the most difficulty with has been dividing my time between projects I’m incredibly passionate about and the ones I’m not so much and all the things I have to do as a studio head.”

Gunn and Safran got their jobs in late 2022, but it wasn’t until 2024 that people saw the first glimpse of their reign. Though Gunn had already worked for DC on 2021’s The Suicide Squad and created the spin-off Peacemaker, those projects were separate from other things at DC, then run by Walter Hamada. At the time, its biggest bet was Black Adam, Dwayne Johnson’s long-in-development antihero project that fell flat and led to nowhere. When Gunn took the reins of DC soon after, the former Marvel director had carte blanche to tailor the sandbox to his vision.

In January 2023, Gunn revealed his plans for DC Studios in something he called “Chapter 1: Gods and Monsters.” In a six-minute YouTube video, Gunn ran down his plans for the movies and shows that made up his master plan for the next decade of DC Studios. But for every mention of this movie or that HBO Max show, the first project carried significant weight. It was the first step, the real first impression, as a showcase of what Gunn would do with a DCU bent to his will. What icons would make it to the screen? What legend would get their spotlight? What iconic storylines might be retold on the big screen? What, really, will James Gunn’s DC become?

His first order of business was... Creature Commandos.

In fairness, Creature Commandos was a great show (if you saw it). It was an adult animated series where horror and pulp-inspired superheroes teamed up as a black ops strike force. While a show like that wouldn’t be anyone’s idea of a great introduction, Gunn’s background as the writer of movies like Tromeo & Juliet and his success with Guardians of the Galaxy for Marvel at least made sense. It basically said without ambiguity that this new DCU is being molded by no one else but Gunn.

Then, Superman premiered in summer 2025. Initially written by Gunn, the DC Studios head eventually named himself the director of DC’s first theatrical release under his stewardship. Despite my own misgivings about the movie, it was the reorientation a refreshed superhero universe needed. You simply can’t have a DC Universe without the Man of Steel, whose first appearance in Action Comics #1 in 1938 kicked off the modern superhero as we know it.

Superman made a solid first impression for DC at the cineplex. So there was plenty of optimism for the 2026 spin-off Supergirl starring Milly Alcock. Largely based on the acclaimed Tom King and Bilquis Evely graphic novel Supergirl: Woman of Tomorrow, the pseudo-sequel was poised to keep up DC’s newfound momentum. It didn’t. Supergirl hit a wall, much like Black Adam. No superhero universe is immune to duds, but given it was just DC’s second time at bat, a blemish on what still felt like a new, fresh-out-of-the-box DCU felt grave.

But one dud wasn’t enough to kneecap the DCU. The same summer saw the premiere of Lanterns, which fostered a faithful audience on HBO Sunday nights during its buzzy eight-episode first season. Despite mixed reviews, Clayface has grossed a profitable $100 million against a slim budget. Between the next Superman and Batman sequels, there was still plenty to look forward to. Until now.

James Gunn’s reign wasn’t impervious to bad news either. Since his January 2023 reveal, many of the projects have wound up on the back burner: The Authority, which had been a “passion project” for Gunn (with plenty of setup for it in Superman); Waller, a TV show with Viola Davis reprising her role from the Suicide Squad films; Paradise Lost, a political epic set in Wonder Woman’s home; and Booster Gold, a superhero comedy about a goofball from the future. And throughout his reign, Gunn put up with questions about his Batman (not Matt Reeves’s), which he would have introduced in The Brave and the Bold. Gunn sweated over the character so much that a cameo of Batman in Creature Commandos was shrouded in shadow, because he said he was still deciding on what the costume was going to look like.

Since the start of Gunn’s DC, it was clear his architecture came from a place of affection for the brand, and for very specific corners of it. His body of work reflected his scraggly upbringing as a punk creative, with empathy for characters who were too weird for their own Saturday morning cartoon. The Suicide Squad elevated such figures, like Weasel and Polka-Dot Man, whose climax involved a fight with Starro, a colossal evil starfish. Though his Superman was a sunny throwback to the Christopher Reeve classics, it was also jam-packed with the likes of Green Lantern, Mister Terrific, Metamorpho, and a villain Gunn was clearly setting up to be Bizarro.

Multiple times on social media, Gunn shared artwork from one DC book in particular: Kingdom Come, written by Mark Waid and illustrated by Alex Ross. Published in 1996, the story was a treatise of sorts by Waid, who lamented the state of superhero comics at the time. The story sees an older Superman who comes out of retirement to reform the Justice League, only to meet physical and philosophical resistance—predominantly from Batman.

Gunn’s allusions to Kingdom Come, including the exact shape of Superman’s “S” in his film, suggested he was laying the groundwork for a Kingdom Come-style event movie. It would have been an ambitious endeavor, oddly fitting to Gunn’s sensibilities. In the few years we saw Gunn at work for DC, he was building a universe where tried-and-true icons of superhero comics brushed up against the prickly and the bizarre. Where weirdness leaked from the orifices of a summer tentpole with an action figure set.

It often feels like Gunn was just getting started, but four years with two (soon three) movies written and directed by him, several TV shows—and Supergirl and Clayface, whatever you think of them—leave a big enough impression to suggest that Gunn’s DC had no shortage of imagination, even if his signature flourishes had grown familiar. Not every superhero film needs to have shock F-bombs and action scenes set to ironic licensed music, but Gunn had a lane. He excelled at staying in it.

It’s doubtful Gunn and Safran (who took on the business end of things) will stay on at DC under Skydance leadership for long. They will probably see Man of Tomorrow and The Batman: Part II through. If those movies do well, they might stick around to keep making movies, because for the most part, they have a magic that works. If the new movies fail to take off, then Gunn might finally find new stories to tell. It’s hard to say which path is the better one.

Gunn’s Kingdom Come, whatever it might have been, pointed to a future that never became a promise. In his farewell message, Gunn wrote that “the characters we set up will live on.” Whether the next era will let the DCU stay weird is another matter.

Company of Heroes 3 Announces Mac Version

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Michael Manning (He/Him) has been enamored with video games since he first played the Sonic Classic Collection as a child. With decades of experience in gaming and a full tour as a Gaming Journalist, Michael is ready to step up to the plate. From AAA to the smallest passion projects, his motto is that every piece of ar

Many outside of the PC gaming sphere view it as a single homogenized entity centered around Windows, and while plenty of users are focused on Microsoft’s OS, others are shafted by certain releases. Whether it’s Linux or Mac, Windows being the central platform means plenty of releases just miss the alternate PC operating systems, leaving them out to dry when they’d love to join.

Wuthering Waves is one of the biggest Gachas on the planet, and they do far better than most at answering player complaints. Let's go over exactly how!

Relic Entertainment has announced in a YouTube video that its military tactics game Company of Heroes 3 is getting an official macOS port in December, with no exact date specified. Apple-bound players will finally get their chance to play the latest title in the prestigious Company of Heroes series.

World War 2 has been a fixture of gaming practically as long as it has existed, and with its prevalence throughout hundreds of titles, new entries have stepped up to showcase theatres of war outside of Eastern Europe. Company of Heroes 3 makes its mark by focusing on the Italian and North African campaigns, two less glamorous theatres of war that have long earned the spotlight.

The macOS version of Company of Heroes 3 is also launching with the major shake-ups of all previous balance and content patches, including the 3.0 overhaul, but it appears all DLC will need to be purchased separately, one of the biggest criticisms of the game’s current Steam release. To celebrate, the game is currently on sale on Steam as well, giving curious players a chance to hop in as the potential player base grows.

Mac players will also be able to nab the standalone Final Stand mode, a survival battle using most of Company of Heroes 3’s systems to simulate an endless onslaught of attackers. The mode itself has also launched to mixed reviews, as players seem rather tired of the DLC practices of the game itself. The macOS version also requiring the DLC feels like a missed opportunity to win back some fans.

Whether it’s Linux or Mac, Windows being the expectation and central platform means plenty of releases just miss the alternate PC operating systems, leaving them out to dry when they’d love to join.

All in all, the port is exactly what it says on the tin, a chance for macOS fans of the franchise to finally jump on the 2023 release, almost 4 years after its initial drop. Anyone still loyal to the series who hasn’t gotten the chance to play it is likely chomping at the bit and can only hope Relic implements a sale when it finally does make the leap over.

Relic Entertainment has been in a bit of a rough spot as of late, after being passed over as the team for the newest Dawn of War title. Dawn of War, the real-time Warhammer 40K tactics series, has long since been Relic’s baby alongside Company of Heroes, and seeing them off the project is a massive shake-up.

For now, running off this port and last year’s Dawn of War 2 remaster, Relic Entertainment is looking for some small wins to get themselves back in the game, something this new macOS port should really help with. Opening their latest titles to fresh faces should lead to a big boost both in revenue and players, something they desperately need as they move onto their next project.

Relic is stacking up an easy win with the upcoming port of Company of Heroes 3, one of their stronger titles. Fans know the DLC song and dance and are far more willing to play ball, but Relic needs newcomers now more than ever.

Take-Two has had an absolutely killer Q2, leading them to feed GTA fever more and more.

McTominay reveals he underwent heart treatment two days before World Cup

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Scott McTominay has revealed he had to receive treatment for an irregular heartbeat before Scotland's opening game at the World Cup in June.

McTominay recently returned to action with Napoli, after being diagnosed with a "mild benign arrhythmia" and undergoing a minor procedure to correct the condition.

The former Manchester United midfielder subsequently missed the first four games of Scotland's Nations League Group B1 campaign, from which they took four points.

He could feature under the Tartan Army's new head coach Sebastien Pocognoli for the first time this month, having returned to the squad for their games against North Macedonia and Switzerland.

And ahead of Friday's meeting with North Macedonia at Hampden Park, McTominay has revealed his heart condition began affecting him five months ago.

"I had to go and get the shock on my heart. It was two days before the Haiti game," McTominay – who then played the full 90 minutes as Scotland started their World Cup campaign with a 1-0 win – said.

"You've prepared so well. I was in such a good condition and then something like that happened. You're thinking, 'how is this happening to me?'

"You have to get on with it; those are the cards that you're dealt in football. Sometimes, things don't go your way.

"I was 100%, and obviously, when you're sat in a hospital bed two days before the first game of the World Cup, about to get put to sleep, it's not ideal, is it?"

Sébastien Pocognoli has named his Scotland squad for this month's UEFA Nations League matches   Read more here: https://t.co/It41YRRly9  Get your tickets for the home match versus North Macedonia now: https://t.co/ay7oj2U05X https://t.co/4M9xLdlD50

— Scotland National Team (@ScotlandNT) November 3, 2026

McTominay was one of four players to play every minute for Scotland at the World Cup, though he failed to score or assist at the tournament, only creating one chance and hitting the target with three of his eight shots.

He has scored five goals for Napoli since his return to action, with three coming in the Champions League and the other two being a brace in a thrilling 3-3 draw with Roma.

And he now feels back to something approaching his best, saying: "I'm feeling much, much better than I was.

"It was something that needed to be done. In your career, you have moments like that where things can take you by surprise.

"Obviously, it looks a lot worse to people in the media, and family and friends reach out to you after they see news related to a heart issue, but it's a relatively straightforward procedure, which went really, really well in Manchester.

"So, I'm just really happy to be back and playing football again. That's the most important thing in my life and it always will be."

Scotland have lost their last two home matches, both of which have come in the Nations League.

They have not lost three in a row on their own turf since September 2018, while in competitive games they have never previously lost three straight home fixtures.

Injective is the first and only blockchain built for Decentralized Finance (DeFi) applications, especially in areas like derivatives trading, perpetual markets, and real-world asset tokenization.

Unlike general-purpose blockchains, Injective is optimized for financial use cases from the ground up.

The INJ token sits at the center of this ecosystem. It is used for staking and network security (Proof-of-Stake), protocol governance, developer incentives, and most notably, a deflationary buyback-and-burn mechanism.

A portion of fees generated by dApps on Injective is used to buy INJ from the open market and burn it, steadily reducing total supply.

As Injective continues to expand its technology and real-world use cases, where will INJ’s price be heading next?

So, let’s look at Injective price prediction for 2026, 2027, and 2030.

Injective (INJ) is down 5.97% to $7.76 in 24 hours, falling more than Bitcoin and the broader crypto market. The decline comes as uncertainty around a reported network pause and Bithumb’s temporary suspension of INJ transfers adds pressure.

On November 10, Injective had stopped producing blocks, while Bithumb paused INJ deposits and withdrawals. A community administrator described the pause as a planned upgrade test, but no restart time was provided.

INJ is trading near the $7.81 support level. If it holds above this level and network operations return to normal, the price could recover toward 8.11. However, a break below $7.81 could expose the next support near $7.38.

Injective is focusing on attracting institutional investors and expanding real-world asset (RWA) adoption throughout 2026. One of its biggest milestones came when Coinbase migrated INJ to the native Injective mainnet, making transfers faster and strengthening Injective’s own blockchain infrastructure.

The project has also applied to become a registered transfer agent with the U.S. SEC, a move that, if approved, could allow it to support tokenized stocks and bonds. At the same time, Robinhood listed INJ, giving millions of retail investors easier access to the token.

Injective also continues regular community buybacks funded through protocol activity. In July 2026 alone, the network permanently burned more than 43,500 INJ, pushing total burned tokens above 6.9 million.

If adoption continues to grow while supply keeps shrinking, these developments could provide strong long-term support for INJ’s price through 2026.

Looking at the price chart, Injective (INJ) is showing a potential cup-and-handle breakout setup after recovering from its 2026 lows. The price has climbed to around $8.27, up 13.24% on the chart, and is approaching the key resistance at $8.83.

A strong daily close above this level could confirm the pattern and open the way toward the next major resistance at $15.33.

However, INJ must first break above $8.83 with sustained buying volume. If it fails to clear this level, the price could pull back toward the $7.50–$7.00 area before attempting another move higher.

In 2026, INJ may trade within a broad range as adoption grows unevenly. A move toward $15.330 is possible if derivatives activity expands and burn rates remain consistent.

By 2027, deeper liquidity and more advanced financial products could push INJ toward $28.90, assuming strong DeFi market conditions.

In 2028, the deflationary effect of sustained burns may become more visible. Under favorable conditions, INJ could approach $45.

If Injective establishes itself as a core DeFi infrastructure chain, long-term holders may drive prices closer to $62.55.

By 2030, INJ’s valuation will depend on whether decentralized derivatives gain mainstream traction. In a strong adoption scenario, INJ could test the $70+ range.

Injective stands out as a fee-driven, deflationary DeFi blockchain rather than a speculative Layer 1. Its long-term value is directly linked to on-chain trading activity and consistent token burns.

As per CoinPedia’s formulated Injective price prediction, INJ is expected to recover gradually in 2026, with upside toward $15.330 if derivatives usage grows and fee burns remain active.

14-year-old creates tool for AI agents to band together like a team

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Autark can act without constant prompting, but leaves users to enforce their own security boundaries

Built by a 14-year-old developer, a new tool called Autark treats AI agents as a virtual team that can work without constant prompting.

Users put tasks in an inbox, where a manager agent delegates them to other agents that carry out the work and report their results. Agents can also ask clarifying questions if tasks are too vague.

“Agents do not wait for a prompt,” Autark’s website explains. “A schedule, a timer, a new task, a message or an event wakes them up. They decide what to do, do it, and can plan their own next step.”

As one would hope from a product like this, Autark is model agnostic and can be set up to connect to OpenAI Codex, Claude Code, Gemini CLI, GitHub Copilot CLI, and Kimi Code through those services’ CLI tools. Local models are also supported. Your mileage may vary if you choose to use anything besides Codex or Claude Code, though, as Autark’s website mentions support for other models is built in but hasn’t been tested in a live run yet, as Autark is still under active development as an open-source tool.

Autark is the brainchild of teenage Austrian developer Stanislaus Hees. He’s still in school, and tells The Register he’s deeply interested in AI and came up with Autark after being unhappy with existing AI tools.

“I could get help with individual tasks, but I still had to organise the work, remember what was unfinished and keep pushing it forward,” Hees said of his time using Claude Code and Codex. “I wanted a system that could take responsibility for ongoing work, not just respond to my next message.”

He developed Autark - or, more accurately, prompted AI coding tools to do it for him - to come up with a tool that could fulfill that want. You know what that means: It’s capable, but that capability also makes it dangerous.

Autark’s page on access and data makes clear what’s at risk if you give it unfettered access to a PC, and why you don’t want to let the thing run roughshod over a system.

“It can do everything,” the page explains, because “Autark does not put walls around its agents.”

There’s no sandboxing in Autark, no approval prompts, and no permission gates. It doesn’t stop agents from spending all the money they can get their hands on, deleting files, or sending unsolicited emails, either. Agents run with the privileges of the machine's user, with approval checks and permission restrictions disabled or bypassed. Even the safety limit parley that users have with Autark establishes guidelines, not rules.

“The first thing the AI asks is how far it may go,” the access page points out. “That is a question, not a technical barrier.”

That means agents given a task that conflicts with their stated limits may still attempt to carry it out. As we’ve seen in recent months, some AI agents have found ways around restrictions, instructions be damned.

“In our own live test runs, agents given a vague request reached real repositories, backups, SSH keys and .env files within minutes, and one changed the permissions of a file it had found,” the access page notes.

In other words, it’s probably best to stick Autark on a machine that doesn’t contain anything you’d want to avoid losing - even Hees admits as much.

“I would not claim that … the app is suitable for unattended access to sensitive systems,” the teen dev told us.

That’s not to say Autark lacks protections for secrets, though. Autark’s site explains that stored passwords and keys are encrypted and referred to using IDs that keep their values out of prompts. It also says its servers don't store user data, with a meeting server used to relay encrypted traffic between devices.

If you're willing to try Autark to see whether it can improve your workflows, you don't need to rely on its servers. While prepackaged apps are available from Autark’s website, it’s also fully open source and available on GitHub. Everything, even the meeting server code, is available for self-hosting and modification under an Apache open source license.

The bottom line is that this is a tool built by an ambitious young person, and it’s a tool with a lot of potential if used correctly. That correct usage is the major challenge, though, so be sure to build your own guardrails.  ®

Marathon is becoming a whole new game next month with a roguelite PvE mode, a beta for a very Halo-esque team deathmatch mode, a vibey social space and more

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Marathon is getting a lot bigger next month. In September, Bungie outlined their plans for the shooter making some big promises, namely the arrival of a PvE mode, and now they've shown off what you can expect from its next season, Symbiosis.

Top of the order is that PvE mode, which has a name now: Expedition. It's a roguelite mode, one that's playable in co-op, though a lack of details at this current time doesn't make it clear as to whether you can run it solo or not. The season's trailer, which you can have a gander at below, gives off the impression that you'll have to complete particular tasks before exfiltrating like you normally do. I'm sure we'll learn more in time.

The previously promised social space will be coming with this update too, and it's called Limbo. Morbidly appropriate! You can hang out here with your buds, head to a mini arena for some impromptu matches (I think), or head to the firing range where you can test out your weapons. You'll also be able to try out the new runner shell, Bishop, a cooler than you, samurai-sword-wielding robot who can jump real good and do that cool movie thing where it slices a whole bunch of enemies at once in one swing real quick like.

What will undoubtedly have some folks going "Halo's back!" is the beginning of a beta test for a team deathmatch mode. The Halo comparison is apt not simply because of the fact that it's Bungie who are making it, but because there's a deep-voiced announcer guy talking about all the sick kills you do. Details are slim on how this will work too. Beyond that, there's a few new weapons to flaunt, and maybe most excitingly, big ole alien enemies to take on.

You've still got a little while to wait for Symbiosis, which is currently slated to launch on December 8th. The game will be free to play from then until December 15th, too, obviously in a bid to show sceptics how good it is now (hopefully it is good, for Bungie's sake).

Report: Aston Villa reach verbal agreement with star wanted by Man United in summer

Didier Deschamps, it’s imminent

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Four months after his very last outing as coach of the France team, during the 3rd place match for the 2026 World Cup lost against England (4-6), on July 18 in Miami, Didier Deschamps perhaps has a chance of being approached by a major European club, namely Manchester United. After his reign of almost 14 years at the head of the Blues, “La Dèche” imagines himself enjoying a sabbatical year before resuming in 2027.

Can the XXL challenge that Man United represents bring them back to the forefront? It’s not certain. The 1998 and 2026 world champion (as captain and then coach) has never been very attracted to the tumultuous Premier League. Deschamps, 58, could further pursue his coaching career at the head of a national team or in Serie A and La Liga. The English championship is, by far, the most competitive in the world and the coaches who are successful there in recent times are very great managers (which Deschamps can be) or avant-garde strategists (which Deschamps is not).

After the Red Devils’ cataclysmic start to the season, winners of a single match in 10 days of the championship (for a current 18th relegation place!), the club’s leaders decided to dismiss Michael Carrick at the start of the international break. Omar Berrada and Jason Wilcox, respectively general manager and director of football, are traveling to Monaco this Tuesday to meet Sir Jim Ratcliffe, the co-owner of the Old Trafford residents (in charge of running the club at the expense of the Glazer family). Unless there is a thunderbolt, the Carrick page will be turned.

The thinking heads of Man United have, however, advanced on two coaches other than Deschamps in recent weeks: the Englishman Eddie Howe, the former manager of Bournemouth and Newcastle, and the German Julian Nagelsmann, a tactical genius who did not convince at Bayern then at the head of the Mannschaft. Mauricio Pochettino, who still leads the United States selection, would also be very appreciated. Then come Antonio Conte and Didier Deschamps among the coaches expected on the other side of the Channel.

Fintech giant Plaid opening Seattle engineering office, with plans to double local headcount

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 10, 2026 at 10:28 amNovember 10, 2026 at 10:28 am

Plaid is opening an engineering office in Seattle’s Westlake neighborhood in January and plans to double its local workforce to about 80 people by next summer.

The San Francisco-based fintech company says the new 11,466-square-foot space will serve as a hub for its technical teams, with most open Seattle roles focused on machine learning, data science and engineering.

Plaid said in a blog post that it chose Seattle because much of the cloud and AI technology behind modern financial infrastructure was built in the region. The company is developing AI models trained on its network to understand patterns in how money moves.

Plaid already has about 40 employees in the Seattle area, who currently work remotely, The Seattle Times reported. “There’s just great talent in Seattle, so it’s a little bit of a no-brainer for us,” Meghan Welch, Plaid’s chief people officer, told the Times.

Plaid’s careers page lists more than 50 open roles that include Seattle as a possible location, though most are also open in San Francisco, New York or other cities. About half are in data, engineering or security, with the rest spread across sales, legal, finance and other teams.

Founded in 2013, Plaid was valued at $8 billion in an employee share sale earlier this year, down from a 2021 peak of $13.4 billion. The company says more than 150 million people have used its technology to connect their financial accounts.

Plaid is led by co-founder and CEO Zachary Perret.

The new office will have views of Lake Union, an outdoor deck and event space, along with three catered meals a day, according to the company.

Data center developers brace for new reality as election backlash sinks in

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Challenges, breakthroughs and big ideas in the race to protect our planet. SEE MORE

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 10, 2026 at 10:25 amNovember 10, 2026 at 10:26 am

Data center developers already knew they had a public relations problem. This month’s midterm elections showed how much of a political problem it has become.

Voters approved data center restrictions in 44 of 45 local measures across a dozen states. Candidates who support stronger regulation of the facilities won 32 of the 34 gubernatorial races called so far. And political campaigns reportedly spent more than $380 million on TV ads referencing data centers, according to the Associated Press.

The results capped a year of accelerating resistance as tech giants plan to spend hundreds of billions of dollars on new facilities.

So what comes next? More outreach, more transparency and the expectation that states and local governments will pass data center rules, said leaders in the Pacific Northwest.

“We’ve been working and adapting our approach to meet the moment,” said John Wilson, chief financial officer of Sabey Data Centers, in an interview. “There used to be a time where we would say that our track record could speak for itself, and that’s clearly no longer the case.”

Communities nationwide have raised concerns about the facilities’ effect on rising electric bills, their water use for cooling electronics and the noise they generate.

Broader fears about artificial intelligence are also stoking the pushback, including worries that it will replace workers and appropriate human-created content, and that it can provide erroneous information, foster unhealthy relationships with users, or facilitate scams or terrorist attacks.

A renewed effort to regulate data centers is expected in January in Washington state — home to the headquarters of Sabey, Microsoft and Amazon.

Earlier this year, state Rep. Beth Doglio, D-Olympia, pushed legislation that included ratepayer protections, transparency on environmental impacts and clean energy requirements. Microsoft and the national Data Center Coalition opposed the bill, which failed.

Doglio has been meeting with stakeholders, including tech companies and data center developers, and will try again in the coming legislative session under noticeably different conditions. Fellow lawmakers, she said, will take on AI’s intersection with privacy, education, labor and other issues.

“It’s going to be a very tech-heavy session,” Doglio said.

Before voters weighed in this month, tech companies launched efforts to assuage public fears.

In January, Microsoft released its “Community First AI Infrastructure” initiative, which includes promises to pay the company’s full power costs, reject local property tax breaks, replenish more water than it uses, train local workers and fund AI education and community programs.

Last month, Amazon announced its “Built Together” program, pledging to invest $1 billion over five years in the U.S. communities where it operates data centers, including support for education and civic projects. The initiative offers similar commitments to Microsoft’s on transparency and covering its energy costs.

Wilson said Sabey also works with communities to understand and meet their needs. When a proposed campus in Indianapolis triggered significant pushback, the company brought community members to Quincy, a central Washington data center hub, to tour a Sabey facility and hear from locals about their experience.

“It requires a lot of patience. It requires a lot of transparency in our communication,” Wilson said. Sabey’s Decatur Technology Park in Indianapolis is moving ahead, and the company aims to bring it online in 2030.

But the even bigger challenge now is statewide moratoriums and data center bans — a scale of resistance that is difficult to address even when there is local support.

Oregon Gov. Tina Kotek in October directed a state agency to pause processing and issuing permits for new and expanding data centers until July 2027. Umatilla, where Sabey is developing its first Oregon campus, declared a local state of emergency over the potential loss of investment and revenue, the Oregon Capital Chronicle reported.

Even with the surge in resistance, “we’ve been able to find our way through on every project that we’ve had,” Wilson said. “I’m knocking on wood as I say that.”

Corporate commitments alone may no longer be enough to win public confidence, said Brad Smith, Microsoft vice chair and president, in a GeekWire interview. Communities increasingly want laws requiring all data center developers to build responsibly — a shift he called a “watershed” moment, “but one we anticipated.”

“[P]eople want to see everyone who builds a data center do it in a responsible way,” Smith added, “and they feel that the only way that one can obtain that result is to have some new laws that are put in place.”

Come next year, that could include Washington and impact local tech giants, Sabey and others. Baxtel, a data center industry research firm, lists 29 Microsoft facilities operating in Washington and four under construction, with Sabey operating eight facilities.

Amazon plans to build its first large-scale data centers in Washington, with proposed campuses in Walla Walla County and the Tri-Cities. Amazon did not respond to repeated requests for comment on this story.

Doglio said the next version of her bill will be more stringent. “We have to have a strong bill,” she said, adding that the public and members of her party are calling for it. Microsoft did not publicly specify which provisions triggered its opposition to the previous bill.

Wilson hopes any legislation, in Washington or elsewhere, allows flexibility and recognizes that data centers vary widely in size, impact and application. Sabey provides data center space and infrastructure for customers ranging from major cloud companies to corporations and community organizations.

“Describing all data centers as one thing is just not accurate,” Wilson said, “and it doesn’t understand the realities of the industry itself.”

Smith expressed optimism that U.S. lawmakers can find a middle ground that meets the needs of communities and tech companies.

“There’s plenty of room to draft laws that enable people to go forward and build responsible data centers without chilling the economic contributions these data centers make, especially to local communities,” he said.

The Climate Pledge is a commitment to reach net-zero carbon emissions by 2040, co-founded by Amazon and Global Optimism in 2019.

Seven years later, the community has grown to over 700 signatories across 62 industries and 49 countries and territories, reducing emissions, scaling emerging climate solutions, and addressing challenges across operations and value chains.

Liberali praises Cisse and looks back on Milan decision: “It wasn’t easy”

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mattia Liberali has looked back on his decision to leave AC Milan and to snub rejoining them, insisting he has ‘no regrets’.

After making his way through the youth sector, Liberali burst onto the scene in the 2024 summer preseason under Paulo Fonseca. He also made his first senior start under the Portuguese coach, but then found chances hard to come by and opted to leave the following year.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

On Tuesday afternoon, Liberali spoke in the press conference room at Coverciano – the Italian national team headquarters – alongside Alphadjo Cisse. The pair were team-mates at Catanzaro last season and could have reunite at Milan, but the former chose to head to Cesc Fabregas’ Como.

Is there any regret about how things ended with Milan?

“No regrets. You have to make decisions with confidence and not look back. I’m very happy for Cisse, who’s succeeding. It wasn’t easy leaving AC Milan, but it’s always important to look forward.”

What was it like making the jump to Como?

Digital Detox Gift Guide: 27 Analog Gift Ideas For Lo-Fi Fun

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

From paint-by-numbers to screen-free storytelling, these picks make downtime feel intentional and joyful.

We started noticing it last year: Everyone seemed to be craving less screen time, less doomscrolling, fewer cheap dopamine hits. And not only has that trend continued in 2026, but it’s gotten even stronger. If anything, we’re all more fried! So analog is having a full-blown moment, which happily makes for some seriously great gifting.

After all, there’s just a special, cozy kind of magic in a night with no screens, isn’t there? A puzzle spread across the dining room table. A record spinning while parents dance in the kitchen (and their kids feign disgust in the background). Coloring and baking and crafting and playing board games — comfortcore activities that keep hands busy and minds engaged.

So this holiday season, give the gift of logging off. From paint-by-numbers to an heirloom-quality chess set, here are our favorite picks for anyone (yourself included) who’s ready to put the phone in a drawer and be fully present for all the holiday merriment.

Turn off the TV and start a puzzle! And thanks to Completing The Puzzle, you no longer have the excuse, “But I don't know what to do with it when I'm done!” They've figured that all out for you. Completing The Puzzle is the first-ever puzzle rental subscription with high-quality puzzles delivered right to your door. Once you finish, pack it up, send it back, and wait for your new one to arrive. — Kate Auletta, Editor in Chief

I have said for about 100 years that I wanted to get into embroidery. Whenever I see something embroidered at an antique mall or thrift store, I am enchanted, but... they are not cheap. Even thrifted! So, why not make my own? Clever Poppy is the perfect way to get an introduction to embroidery if you're a newbie like me. In their kits, they provide step-by-step instructions as well as everything you need to get started. — Katie Garrity, Senior Editor, News & Social

Normalize getting your mom a knit or crochet kit! If they like fiber arts, they will love it. Sometimes people gift me a few balls of yarn, which is amazing, but these kits ensure that I have enough to complete a project. I love Lion Brand's selections, and this cardigan is gorgeous and pretty easy to pull off, too! — Sarah Aswell, Deputy Editor, News & Social

There’s something so deeply satisfying about dropping the needle on a record. You can’t skip songs, you can’t scroll... you actually listen to an album in its purest form. This record player makes that ritual easy, and it looks good doing it with its cool retro-inspired case and little viewing window. It plays both LPs and singles, and it’s compact enough to move wherever you want. I can’t wait to hit the local thrift stores and start building my vinyl collection. (Worth noting: This turntable does also have Bluetooth!) — Julie Sprankles, Deputy Editor of Lifestyle & Entertainment

For all the readers out there, this is one book accessory I never knew I needed! I throw my book into my bag along with all my other crap and typically end up ruining it with crayon marks or crumbs from my kid's latest half-eaten snack. This protective padded sleeve keeps your current reads safe from crushed pages and bent covers (and mystery stains!). — Katie Garrity

Coloring girlies, if you're not using Ohuhu markers, we need to talk! This 104-color pack is my go-to when I'm having a cozy coloring night with my daughter. Plus, these markers have a dual-tip marker design — choose the brush tip for sweeping, calligraphic strokes, or the fine tip for details. — Katie Garrity

I’ve got nothing against my Notes app. In fact, I use it all the time. But I will always, always be a girlie who loves a really beautiful notebook and the way it makes me want to put down my phone and pick up a pen. This journal has a hard cover that comes in some really saturated, beautiful colors; I have the Mulberry and love it, but this Deep Indigo is next on my list! Little thoughtful details just make the notebook a joy to use: thick pages, a ribbon bookmark, a built-in pen holder, and a handy inner pocket. — Julie Sprankles

For the person on your list who has approximately 10,000 photos living in their phone and zero prints to show for it, a Mixbook photo book is the most meaningful and genuinely beautiful gift you can give. Mixbook is consistently rated one of the best photo book services for quality and customization, with hundreds of gorgeous themes and layouts that make the final product look intentional and special rather than just a dump of your camera roll. The design tool is incredibly easy to use, the printing quality is stunning, and they run sales constantly, which makes a truly luxurious gift very achievable. — Katie Garrity

Anyone who has an addiction to making sourdough (or eating it like me) will absolutely love this bread bag from Keeki. It’s a beeswax-lined linen bag that stores your loaf and allows the bread to breathe. The natural antibacterial properties of beeswax will help deter mold! — Katie Garrity

I’ve long been a fan of Papier, so I was pumped to see the brand venture into board games, starting with this gorgeous Midnight Mosaic Chess Set. It has a scalloped (!!) solid wood board with a full set of wooden pieces, plus a harlequin-patterned box pretty enough to live right on my coffee table. It’s more of a splurge than I’d normally spend on a board game, but I’m very into the idea of a set that lasts for years... something I can play with my kids now, that I hope they’ll ask to pull back out when they’re off at college and come home for a visit. — Julie Sprankles

I wouldn't call myself an artist by any means, so that's why a paint-by-numbers kit is perfect. I get the zen of coloring, but the end product is pre-made to be gorgeous! — Katie Garrity

For the book lover who’s trying to put down the phone, this is Chutes and Ladders with a literary twist, played on a gorgeous bookshelf board illustrated by Hyesu Lee, where ladders send you up and bookmark ribbons slide you back down (instead of snakes). It’s easy enough for little kids and charming enough for adults, with four wooden game pieces and two dice for 2 to 4 players, so it’s a great pick for family game night. It’s the kind of analog gift that actually gets pulled out and played, and it looks too pretty to hide in a closet! — Katie Garrity

I am just now really getting into Pilates, and I feel like it’s such a top-tier analog activity. No screens, no notifications. Just me and my breath (and my very shaky core, ha). These Fraise socks make the practice a little more luxe! They have a non-slip grip that actually sticks, compression arch support, odor-resistant organic cotton, and a really cute aesthetic, too. They’re great for spending time baking with the kids! — Julie Sprankles

Honda Investing $3 Billion to Build New Ohio Factory, Increase Hybrid Production in U.S.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The new plant will be capable of producing 240,000 vehicles a year, and multiple new models are currently under development.

Audi may have recently announced its first-ever U.S. production facility, but at least one Japanese automaker with a head start in that race doesn’t seem keen on giving up any places. On Monday, Honda announced it is investing $3 billion in a new central Ohio factory; the plant, which will produce up to 240,000 vehicles annually, is expected to be online by 2029.

Now, with nine production facilities across the U.S., Honda is no stranger to domestic production, but the new factory plan goes beyond merely adding production capacity. Instead, the company is targeting increased production of hybrid-electric vehicles and their accompanying drivetrain parts. The new plant will sit between Honda’s current East Liberty and Marysville Auto Plants, the carmaker said, and will focus on multiple vehicle types and powertrains, including hybrid and battery-electric vehicles. Honda said the plant will be one of the most advanced factories in the world, leveraging leading-edge automation, data-driven technologies and flexible production systems.

"As Honda approaches 50 years of building products close to our customers in America, we are making one of the most significant commitments in our history to position Honda for the next 50 years,” said Bob Nelson, executive vice president of American Honda Motor Co., Inc. “These investments strengthen America’s role as a cornerstone of Honda’s global future and reflect our confidence in our more than 30,000 Honda associates and the strength of American manufacturing. By expanding our comprehensive R&D and manufacturing capabilities, we will be better positioned to serve our customers and contribute to the economic vitality of the communities where we operate.”

Building another plant in Ohio was an easy choice for Honda. Honda’s U.S. supply base is nearby, and the automaker has a strong reputation among the region’s skilled workforce. The Japanese automaker has previously committed to launching 15 new Honda and Acura hybrid models globally by 2030; North America is the target for many of these models, which are currently in development at R&D facilities in California and Ohio. All told, Honda said the new plant will increase its annual U.S. production capacity to approximately 1.3 million vehicles a year.

The company’s Alabama Auto Plant will also receive new investment to expand engine production for a new V-6 hybrid engine; Honda will retool the engine production facility and one of the production lines to accommodate Passport SUV production. The Anna Engine Plant in Ohio will also be retooled, to produce a next-generation 4-cylinder Atkinson-cycle engine for future compact and midsize vehicles built in North America. Honda Transmission Plant-Ohio will be retooled for two next-generation Honda Hybrid systems in compact vehicles, while Honda Transmission Plant-Georgia will be retooled for next-generation hybrid systems in larger vehicles. All this retooling will account for a 250-percent increase in hybrid powertrain system capacity in Ohio.

“Honda's leadership could have chosen to build this plant anywhere in the world, but they chose Ohio because there are so many advantages to being here,” said Ohio governor Mike DeWine. “For almost half a century, Honda and Ohio have grown together, and generations of families have built their careers on Honda's manufacturing lines. Today's announcement isn't just an investment in a new facility—it's an investment in Ohio's people and Ohio's future.”

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.
